1 Introduction

DOC-1778 · section

1.1 What NeoTeletext is

DOC-1790 · concept · informative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-299, REQ-1643, REQ-1732

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1732 One single published protocol document defines NeoTeletext's entire protocol: upstream, downstream, teletext rendering, audio and everything else (not implemented on main).

Verified by inspection. REQ-299 World System Teletext numbering only: Read at engine 6112177: `crates/neoteletext-core/src/limits.rs` is the one definition of magazines, page numbers and sub-codes, in EN 300 706's layout alone, and `packet/header.rs` decodes only that form. No test can show that a thing is absent: the requirement forbids the addressing of other systems, and it is checked by reading that none is there.

NeoTeletext is teletext carried by the web. A viewer opens a channel by its address, reads numbered pages of 40 columns by 25 rows, moves between them by page number and by coloured links, and on some channels types, points and plays. Everything on the screen is a teletext page, coded and drawn by the rules of World System Teletext. It is not a web page: there is no script, no bitmap and no layout engine, and a page's dimensions never change.

What a page means is defined by EN 300 706, which NeoTeletext follows and cites. How pages reach a receiver over the internet is NeoTeletext's own, and is defined here: a static channel is a folder of files on a web server, and a live channel adds one WebSocket that carries teletext packets down and the viewer's input up.

This document is the whole of that definition. A developer can build a client, a server, a channel or a program from it and the standards it links to. It is generated from a database of requirements and must not be edited; each part names the requirements it states.

This version is a draft.

1.2 A channel

DOC-1791 · concept · informative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-507

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-507 Channels: a channel is a folder (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

A channel is a teletext service on the web: a folder at an http or https address whose path ends in /. Its identity is its origin and its folder path, compared exactly, so two folders of one web server are two channels and share nothing. That identity is the boundary for cookies, redirects and links.

A broadcast sends every page round and round and a receiver captures the few it wants. A channel keeps each page at a fixed address and a receiver asks for the few it wants. The files of a channel have fixed names worked out from page numbers, so a folder copied to any web server, or read from a disk, is the same channel.

1.3 A page and a sub-page

DOC-1792 · concept · informative · requirements REQ-300, REQ-304, REQ-418, REQ-1643

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-300 Every address the standard defines is accepted (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-418 The receiver-owned status area cannot be forged (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

A page is addressed by a magazine, 1 to 8, and a page number of two hexadecimal digits, and written as three digits with the magazine first: 100, 1FE. A viewer keys only the 800 decimal numbers from 100 to 899. The hexadecimal numbers are reached by links and mostly carry data.

A sub-page is one version of a page, told apart by its thirteen-bit sub-code, written as four hexadecimal digits from 0000 to 3F7E. A sub-page is a page in every way that matters: it is selected, sent, updated and linked on its own. 3F7F is not a sub-page: in an address it means "no particular sub-page".

A page is always 40 columns by 25 rows, numbered from 0. Row 0 is the header. Columns 0 to 7 of row 0 are the receiver's own status area, which no page can write.

Defined by: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 7.2 Elements of a page; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 9.3.1.1 Page number; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 9.3.1.2 Page sub-code

1.4 Receivers, servers and programs

DOC-1793 · concept · informative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-1474, REQ-1501

A receiver is anything that opens a channel and shows its pages: the NeoTeletext browser, the command-line program neottx, or a microcontroller with a screen. A receiver decodes the same packets a television decoder did, into the same 40 by 25 grid.

A server answers a channel's address. For a static channel any web server will do. For a live channel the NeoTeletext server holds the WebSocket and passes messages between its viewers and a program. It keeps no content of its own beyond what a connection needs.

A program is the code behind a live channel, in any language. It runs outside the server, publishes pages, and hears what each viewer selects and types. The kits for Python and TypeScript wrap that protocol.

A generator is anything that writes a channel's files.

1.5 Live and static channels

DOC-1794 · concept · informative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-494, REQ-507, REQ-1462

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-507 Channels: a channel is a folder (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-1462 Only packets of the allowed kinds downstream; a receiver discards, counts and reports the rest (tested, not recorded as met on main).

A static channel is its files and nothing else. A receiver fetches channel.ttx, then a bundle of first sub-pages, then each page's file as it is wanted, with ordinary conditional requests over HTTP. It can be served by any host that serves files, and needs no program.

A live channel says so in channel.ttx. A receiver then opens one WebSocket at the channel's push and fetches no page file: pages, sub-pages and the channel's contents all arrive on that connection as teletext packets, and the selection a viewer makes travels up it. Only a live channel can take a viewer's keys.

The bytes of a page are the same in both. A live connection is a plain stream of 42-byte packets, and what is not a page travels as records inside packets on one data channel, so a recording of a connection plays in any decoder that reads T42.

The smallest channels. A page a viewer can read is a folder holding 100.tti. A page a viewer can use is a channel.ttx of { "name": "Example", "push": { "input": true } } at a folder's address, and a server answering push on that origin with a program that sends page 100 and reads the viewer's keys. A static host cannot serve push, so a channel that takes keys needs a server on its own origin.

1.6 Normative words and how to read this document

DOC-1795 · concept · informative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-1734, REQ-1737

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1734 The protocol document is generated from rdm: its text lives in the database as documentation items and nobody edits the generated file (not implemented on main); REQ-1737 Documentation items are DITA-style typed topics written in a strict XHTML subset, with rdm's terms as the glossary (to be rechecked on main).

The capitalised words MUST, MUST NOT, SHOULD, SHOULD NOT and MAY have the meanings of RFC 2119. They appear only in items marked normative. An item marked informative explains, and no implementation is held to it. In a normative item, a plain statement of what a receiver, a server or a file does has the force of MUST, unless it says otherwise, as with "by default" or "a setting allows".

Every item is marked with what kind of text it is: a concept explains, a reference holds facts to look up, a task is a procedure, and a troubleshooting item pairs a problem with its fix.

An item that a standard defines lists the clauses under "Defined by", each a link to the heading in the publisher's own PDF. Such an item does not repeat the standard: read the clause for the definition, and the item for what NeoTeletext does with it, what it chooses where the standard leaves room, and where it departs. An item that no standard defines says so: it is NeoTeletext's own, and it lists under "Rests on" the places of a standard it relies on, which define what it refers to and not the item.

Offsets of a byte example count from the first byte of the example. A teletext packet here is the 42 bytes a T42 file holds, so offset 0 of a packet is byte 4 of the standard's numbering. Bit numbers run from b1, the least significant bit of a byte, as in EN 300 706.

A passage that begins Not built. describes what is required and is not yet in the code. A receiver or server MUST NOT rely on it. What was decided and never built is in informative items whose titles begin "Decided, not built", so a normative item holds only what can be relied on. An item stating a requirement that the records do not hold as implemented says so at its head, and the document ends with an index of such items: that mark says what the records hold, and not that the work is missing. A section with no text has not been written yet.

1.7 The standards this document cites

DOC-1796 · reference · informative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-1733, REQ-1736

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1733 The protocol document links to each standard at ETSI's own URL, pinned by SHA-256, and lands on the cited clause's heading; no standard is copied into a repository (to be rechecked on main).

Four documents are cited, each by its published text at its publisher's address. None is copied here. Each file is pinned by its SHA-256, because a link's page and position belong to that exact file.

DocumentWhat NeoTeletext takes from it
ETSI EN 300 706 V1.2.1, Enhanced Teletext specificationPackets, pages, numbering, presentation at Levels 1 to 3.5, and character sets. The published text is cited, never the final draft.
ETSI EN 300 708 V1.2.1, Data transmission within TeletextThe independent data lines that carry audio and records, and the rules for data broadcasting pages a receiver must not show.
ETSI EN 300 472 V1.3.1, Teletext in DVB bitstreamsHow to read teletext out of a recorded MPEG-2 transport stream.
ETSI TR 100 287 V1.2.1, Code of practice for enhanced TeletextGuidance only: the numbering of sub-pages NeoTeletext's automatic numbering follows.

2 Addressing and the URL map

DOC-1779 · section

2.1 One address does every job

DOC-1907 · concept · informative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-1693

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1693 Every channel has one complete, fixed, defined URL map: a channel is a folder, and a place in it is the end of the path (to be rechecked on main).

A channel has one URL map. It is complete, so nothing is reachable by an address it does not list. It is fixed, so it is the same for every channel, and a channel cannot rename, move or add to it. It is defined, so each thing has one spelling, and every other spelling is normalised to it or refused.

One address does every job: a viewer's address field, a web visitor, a search engine and a link preview. A receiver never asks a server for a page's address. It goes from <channel>200 to the file 200.tti by rule on a static channel, and to a SELECT of 200 on a live one. Only an ordinary web browser or a search engine requests the address itself.

For a page or a sub-page, the same text is what SELECT carries, so an address and a selection cannot disagree. contents and wall are places of an address only: they are never selected, and a SELECT of either closes the connection.

2.2 The places of a channel

DOC-1908 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-512, REQ-1693, REQ-1883, REQ-1897

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-512 Channel addresses (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-1693 Every channel has one complete, fixed, defined URL map: a channel is a folder, and a place in it is the end of the path (to be rechecked on main); REQ-1883 A channel is a folder. (to be rechecked on main).

In this part <channel> means an http or https origin and folder, ending in /, such as https://app.neottx.com/home/. A place is what follows it.

AddressOpens
<channel>The channel, arriving at its initial page
<channel>302Page 302
<channel>302/0002Sub-page 0002 of page 302
<channel>302?name=valuePage 302, with a query for the channel's program
<channel>302/0002?name=valueSub-page 0002 of page 302, with a query
<channel>contentsThe channel's contents
<channel>wallThe wall of pages, with no page selected

Spellings.

  • A page is three hexadecimal digits, the magazine 1 to 8 first.
  • A sub-page is four hexadecimal digits, a sub-code 0000 to 3F7E with no bit outside the thirteen a sub-code has. 3F7F means no particular sub-page, so it cannot name one.
  • Hexadecimal digits are capitals in the one spelling.
  • The words contents and wall are in small letters.
  • A query is name=value pairs joined by &, percent-encoded. A name may not be empty, and a value may.
  • The whole address is at most 8,000 octets.

Where a channel ends. A parser reads the end of the path. A last segment of contents or wall, a last segment that is a page number, or a page number followed by a sub-code is inside the channel, and what comes before it is the channel's folder. Any other address MUST end in /, and is a folder. So a folder named contents, wall or like a page number, such as 100 or 2fa, cannot be a channel: it belongs to the channel above it. Only the end of the path is read, so /100/news/ is a channel.

2.3 The names receivers use

DOC-1909 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-508, REQ-1693, REQ-1884

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-508 The files of a channel (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-1693 Every channel has one complete, fixed, defined URL map: a channel is a folder, and a place in it is the end of the path (to be rechecked on main); REQ-1884 Fixed names, one map. (tested, not recorded as met on main).

These are fixed names in the channel's folder, or in a page's folder inside it. A viewer never sees them in an address field, and a file of a page's folder has the path of the address it holds: <channel>602/0101.t42 holds what <channel>602/0101 names. The files of a channel says what each holds.

NameChannelHolds
channel.ttxLive and staticThe channel's settings
pushLiveThe WebSocket. It is always <channel>push, and carries no query and no page
contents.txtStaticThe contents
index.t42StaticA bundle of pages, first sub-pages only
NNN.tti, NNN.t42StaticA page, or its first sub-page when it has several
NNN/SSSS.t42, NNN/SSSS.ttiStaticA sub-page
NNN/P.subStaticA part of a page's list of sub-pages

A viewer's address naming one of these that is not a page or a sub-page is refused. A command-line reader told to open channel.ttx opens its channel: neottx https://h.example/a/channel.ttx is the channel at https://h.example/a/.

2.4 Normalising an address

DOC-1910 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-512, REQ-1693

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-512 Channel addresses (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-1693 Every channel has one complete, fixed, defined URL map: a channel is a folder, and a place in it is the end of the path (to be rechecked on main).

Each of these is changed to the one spelling when an address is typed, linked, redirected to or opened from outside. An address field, history and bookmarks hold only that spelling.

WrittenBecomes
<channel>, once the channel has given its initial page<channel>NNN for that page, replacing the entry and adding no Back entry. A query goes with it: <channel>?a=1 becomes <channel>100?a=1.
1fe, 302/3f7e1FE, 302/3F7E: capitals
<channel>302/<channel>302: a final slash after a place is removed
<channel>302.tti, <channel>302.t42<channel>302
<channel>302/0002.t42, <channel>302/0002.tti<channel>302/0002

A query is not normalised. It stays in the order given, percent-encoded as it came.

2.5 What is refused

DOC-1911 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-505, REQ-512, REQ-1693

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-512 Channel addresses (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-1693 Every channel has one complete, fixed, defined URL map: a channel is a folder, and a place in it is the end of the path (to be rechecked on main).

An address outside the map MUST NOT be repaired. It is not opened, and a link carrying it is not a link.

  • A fragment on an http or https address is refused, never ignored: <channel>#302, <channel>302#303.
  • A place the map does not list: 30, 3021, G02, 902, 002, 302/2, 302/3F7F, 302/0080, WALL, contents/1.
  • A query that is not name=value pairs: 302?, 302?name, 302?=value, 302?a=1&.
  • A query after contents or wall.
  • The names receivers use that are not pages: channel.ttx, contents.txt, index.t42, 302/0.sub. The names channel.json, 302-0002.t42 and 302.sub are no names of a channel's.
  • Any other file in a folder named as a place, which is not a channel: 302/100.t42, 302/channel.ttx.
  • Any scheme but http and https.

2.6 The conformance table

DOC-1912 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-1693

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1693 Every channel has one complete, fixed, defined URL map: a channel is a folder, and a place in it is the end of the path (to be rechecked on main).

This table has three parts. The addresses that open and the addresses refused are what a program that reads an address does with it: the engine's parser and the browser's address rules run them, and a new reader SHOULD as well. The third part is what a server answers a request for each target, and the server's routes run that: a server is not held to the first two, which what each address answers explains. Its source is the file map/conformance.tsv of neoteletext-web.

Addresses that open, with their one spelling
AddressOne spelling
https://h.example/home/https://h.example/home/
http://h.example/home/http://h.example/home/
https://h.example/https://h.example/
https://h.example/home/302https://h.example/home/302
https://h.example/home/302/0002https://h.example/home/302/0002
https://h.example/home/302?name=valuehttps://h.example/home/302?name=value
https://h.example/home/302/0002?name=valuehttps://h.example/home/302/0002?name=value
https://h.example/home/contentshttps://h.example/home/contents
https://h.example/home/wallhttps://h.example/home/wall
https://h.example/home/?name=valuehttps://h.example/home/?name=value

2.7 The query

DOC-1913 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-1388, REQ-1674, REQ-1693

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1388 The page and sub-page a connection has selected are sent with SELECT, which carries no view number (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-1693 Every channel has one complete, fixed, defined URL map: a channel is a folder, and a place in it is the end of the path (to be rechecked on main).

An address has one query, and it goes with the selection. A receiver sends it in SELECT, and a program receives it, as written, in selected, or in connected for a selection made before the program was told of the connection.

A query belongs to that one selection: choosing another page, by any path, leaves it behind. It stays in the address, so a copied address, a bookmark or Reload sends it again. It MUST NOT go on the push connection's own address. A static channel has no program, so the query stays in the address and does nothing, as a static host ignores one.

The server does not interpret a query. It is untrusted text from the viewer: bytes 0x21 to 0x7E with no #, so no space, at most 8,000 bytes, percent-encoded as it came, and its percent-encoding is not checked.

2.8 What each address answers on a live channel's server

DOC-1914 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-1693

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1693 Every channel has one complete, fixed, defined URL map: a channel is a folder, and a place in it is the end of the path (to be rechecked on main).

A live channel on the NeoTeletext server answers exactly this, and nothing else. The server serves no channel's files: a live channel's pages, contents and sub-pages travel on its connection. Only GET and HEAD reach the small page or a redirect, and another method there is answered 404.

RequestAnswer
<channel>, <channel>contents, <channel>wall, each with any query200, text/html; charset=utf-8: a small page for an ordinary web browser, naming the channel, with a neottx:// link to that address and a link to get NeoTeletext. It runs no script: its Content-Security-Policy is default-src 'none'; base-uri 'none'; form-action 'none'; frame-ancestors 'none'.
<channel>NNN, <channel>NNN/SSSS, in either case, each with any query, not looked at, and with no redirect to capitals200, as describing a page says: the small page with noindex, or a public page. A sub-page shows its page, and a query is not sent to the program and changes nothing but the neottx:// link.
The folder without its final /308, to <channel>, on the same origin, keeping its query
A place with a final /308, to the same place with no final /, in the case it was asked, keeping its query
<channel>channel.ttx200, application/json, Cache-Control: no-cache: the settings, generated: the channel's name, its initial_page and "push":{"input":true}
<channel>pushThe WebSocket; 426 without an upgrade
Anything else under the channel, a page's file name such as 302.tti among them, and /404

A static channel answers whatever its host answers, and a receiver asks it only for the names receivers use.

2.9 Redirects, local files and links from outside

DOC-1915 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-510, REQ-1693

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1693 Every channel has one complete, fixed, defined URL map: a channel is a folder, and a place in it is the end of the path (to be rechecked on main).

Redirects. An HTTP redirect of a channel's channel.ttx moves the channel, and the reader keeps the place it was opening. A program's redirect names a place of its own channel, or a whole address of this map. The two are different things: the first is an HTTP response, and the second a record on the connection.

Links in a page. A link resolves through this map, never through anything a page says about files. A page link, from X/27/0, is NNN in the same channel; name:mpp goes through services; an alias through aliases; and a contents.txt x entry names a channel or a file of one.

Local files. A file: address names a real file, so a path cannot also name a page in it: a file takes the places of this map after #, as file:///tmp/news.tti#200. This is the one use of #. Arriving normalises to the initial page, and a file: address is never split by the file-name rule: each local file is its own channel.

Links from outside. A neottx:// link wraps a whole address of this map and adds nothing to it: neottx://https://h.example/home/302 opens https://h.example/home/302.

Outside the map. The browser's built-in page neoteletext:home is not in a channel.

3 The transport and the session

DOC-1780 · section

3.1 One connection for each viewer's tab

DOC-1916 · concept · informative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-494, REQ-1501

A live channel holds one WebSocket for each tab that shows it. The connection is a pipe, not a request: moving between pages does not reconnect. A viewer's selection is a web request that stays open inside it, answered with a page and then that page's changes until the next selection replaces it.

Each tab has its own connection, decoder and store, never shared with another tab, even one on the same channel. So the server sees one connection as one view with one selection. The channel's cookies stay shared by its tabs, as two tabs of a web site share them.

What travels down is a T42 stream and nothing else, so a recording of a connection plays in any decoder. What travels up is a compact binary stream of the viewer's input, of which SELECT is the one message every receiver needs.

The order of a session is: the upgrade, HELLO, WELCOME, the first selection, its page, and from then on input and changes.

3.2 Opening the connection

DOC-1917 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-494, REQ-524, REQ-1337, REQ-1339, REQ-1895

The connection's address is the channel's address with push added, and with the scheme wss for an https channel and ws for an http one. It carries no query and no page. A receiver opens it only when channel.ttx has a push member. ws is allowed, and a receiver MUST say that it is not secure wherever it says where keys go.

The request is an RFC 6455 upgrade over HTTP/1.1, on a connection of its own that carries nothing else.

  • A receiver MUST send Sec-WebSocket-Protocol: neottx.7: neottx. and the input protocol's version.
  • A receiver MUST NOT send Origin. A web page's script always sends one, so a receiver cannot be written in a web page.
  • A receiver sends the channel's cookies, and a User-Agent. It asks for no extension.

The server's answer. One rule holds for every host.

The upgradeAnswer
Carries Origin, whatever its value, null included403, whatever it offers. This stops a web page reaching a channel with a viewer's cookies.
Does not offer neottx.7426, with Upgrade: websocket and Sec-WebSocket-Protocol: neottx.7, naming the one spoken
Offers it101, echoing Sec-WebSocket-Protocol: neottx.7, with no extension
Not an upgrade at all426

The NeoTeletext server looks at Origin and the offer only when a request asks to upgrade: one that does not is answered 426 with neither Upgrade nor Sec-WebSocket-Protocol, with or without Origin. It does not check the method, the HTTP version, Sec-WebSocket-Version or a query on push, so a client must not rely on being refused for those. A stricter server is allowed.

The offer is a comma-separated list, matched exactly, since a subprotocol's name is case-sensitive. A server MUST NOT read a receiver's agent text to decide anything: capabilities in HELLO are the only signal. The NeoTeletext server sets its session cookie on the 101 response.

The receiver's check. A receiver MUST close the connection and stop when a 101 does not name exactly the subprotocol it asked for, or names an extension. permessage-deflate is never used: private rows compressed in one context with attacker-influenced public rows make a compression oracle.

There is no compatibility path during development: old and new refuse each other at the handshake.

3.3 Messages on the connection

DOC-1918 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-494, REQ-524, REQ-1066, REQ-1332, REQ-1338, REQ-1462

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1462 Only packets of the allowed kinds downstream; a receiver discards, counts and reports the rest (tested, not recorded as met on main).

DirectionMessageBound
DownA binary message of whole 42-byte packets. Joined in the order sent, the messages are a plain T42 file, with nothing between packets and nothing added to them. See the downstream record set.A receiver takes at most 65,536 bytes a message by default, and the server cuts what is longer. A cut falls before a sub-page's header where it can, and otherwise at a packet.
UpA binary message: a piece of the input streamAt most 8,192 bytes
UpA text message: the push subscription, subscribe all, or subscribe and page numbers. The engine's receiver sends it when the connection opens. The NeoTeletext server ignores every text message, and a new receiver need not send one: what a connection is sent is its selection.Small

A server MUST NOT send a text message. A receiver discards one, applies nothing of it, and counts it as one invalid packet. A binary message that is not whole packets is discarded whole, and counts as its length divided by 42, rounded up. Neither closes the connection.

Writing. Each event is its own message, written as it happens. Messages waiting at the same moment are written together, in one write and one TLS record where the transport allows, and nothing is held back to be grouped. Input is never sent in TLS early data or a TCP Fast Open SYN, which could be replayed.

The heartbeat. A receiver pings a connection silent for 25 seconds, and treats one that does not answer within 10 seconds as lost. The server pings a connection silent for 35 seconds, 25 and 10 together, so a live receiver's own ping comes first, and drops one that answers nothing within 10 seconds, without a close. Either side answers a ping with a pong, and any byte heard counts as a sign of life.

The round trip. A receiver measures the connection by ping and pong, at most once a second while keys go to the channel, and not from acknowledgements, which measure only the channel's receipt.

3.4 Close codes

DOC-1919 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-494, REQ-1036

A close code says whether to try again, in two published ranges, so that a code nobody has defined yet needs no change in a receiver.

CodeNameMeaningA receiver
4000CLOSE_BROKE_PROTOCOLThe receiver broke the WebSocket or the input protocolStops
4001CLOSE_WRONG_VERSIONHELLO named a version the channel does not speakStops
4002 to 4099UnnamedReservedStops
4100CLOSE_RESTARTINGThe server is restartingTries again
4101 to 4199UnnamedReservedTries again
Any other, or no close at all1000 among them, and a connection that simply wentTries again

A protocol error closes the connection, on either side, with a code a receiver stops on. A receiver answers a close with a close, as RFC 6455 section 5.5.1 says.

3.5 Stopping and trying again

DOC-1920 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-494, REQ-1036

Stopping. A receiver that stops MUST NOT reconnect until the viewer opens the channel again. It stops on a close code of 4000 to 4099; on an upgrade answered with a status of 400 to 499 other than 429, which trying again would meet again; and on a 101 without the subprotocol it asked for.

Trying again. After any other loss, a 429 and a status of 500 or above among them, a receiver waits a random time in the upper half of a ceiling, and connects again. The ceiling starts at 1 second and doubles to at most 60 seconds, so the waits are 0.5 to 1 second, then 1 to 2, then 2 to 4, and so on. The randomness is so that the viewers a restart sent away do not all come back at once, and the upper half so that none comes back at once by chance.

The ceiling goes back to 1 second once a connection has shown itself healthy, by bringing packets or a pong. A connection that merely opened has not.

A reconnection is a new session, unless it resumes one. Its receiver sends HELLO again, and SELECT, so the channel sends its page again.

3.6 The session

DOC-1921 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-205, REQ-1471, REQ-1481

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1481 Every selection has an ending: when a program does not answer in time, or is not running, the viewer is told the channel is unavailable and the connection stays open (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

  1. When the connection opens, a receiver sends HELLO with version 7 and its capabilities.
  2. The channel answers with WELCOME: the version it speaks and the subset of the capabilities it will use. A channel that cannot speak the version closes the connection with 4001.
  3. The receiver then sends RESIZE, FOCUS_IN or FOCUS_OUT, and SELECT when it shows a page of the channel, and from then on the viewer's input.

A receiver MUST send no input before WELCOME. A channel that has not answered within WELCOME_TIMEOUT, 10 seconds, is taken not to take input, and the receiver says so.

The first SELECT. The server holds its connected to the program until the connection's first SELECT, for at most FIRST_SELECTION_WAIT_MS, 1,500 ms, so that a shared link goes straight to its page with no initial page before it. A SELECT of nothing also ends the hold. A receiver that does not set the SELECT capability is not waited for, and is shown the program's answer for the initial page.

A selection's ending. Every selection ends with a page, or with DENIED, within PROGRAM_REPLY_LIMIT_MS, 5,000 ms, of the SELECT. After that the server sends DENIED 503 itself, with the channel's program running or not, and at once when no program is running. So a receiver needs no timer of its own for a selection. A later page for the same selection is still shown: the selection stands, and the program has come back. A selection a newer one replaced has no ending of its own, and what the program sends for it is dropped.

Protocol versions. Both sides speak 7, and there is no compatibility path. Version 3 added the stamp to input events, 4 the REDIRECT record, 5 a query in SELECT, 6 SELECT's payload as the address's text, and 7 the subprotocol and the close codes, and a query in a REDIRECT's place.

A reconnection is a new session: HELLO again, nothing held, and the input count from zero. The channel sends the selected page again when it receives SELECT.

3.7 Capabilities

DOC-1922 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-205, REQ-1556

The caps field of HELLO and WELCOME is a bit set. A sender uses a new flag bit, field, core type or extension type only when both sides set its capability. There are no input modes: every event is sent, and a channel ignores what it does not use. Bits 0 to 15 were input modes until 27 October 2026, and are reserved, never set, and ignored when a peer still sets them.

BitValueCapabilityMeaning
1665,536NoneRetired, PAGE. Never set, and a protocol error when set in HELLO or WELCOME.
17131,072CURSORThe receiver draws the channel's cursor record.
18262,144NoneRetired, provisional echo. Never set, and ignored when a peer still sets it.
19524,288AGENTHELLO carries the user agent string. In HELLO only, never in WELCOME.
201,048,576TOUCHThe receiver sends touch: MOUSE's device bits and TOUCH. Without it touch goes as the mouse, the first contact alone.
212,097,152RESUMEA lost connection's session may be resumed.
224,194,304SELECTThe receiver shows teletext pages and sends SELECT. A channel that sets it sends the one sub-page selected, never a page's sub-pages together.

A receiver that does not set SELECT has no selection of its own: it is shown the program's answer for the channel's initial page. Cursor records are the one exception to the rule above: they carry the acknowledgement, so they go to every receiver, and CURSOR says only that the receiver draws them. WELCOME's capabilities are a subset of HELLO's, and a receiver refuses a WELCOME that sets any other.

Not built. The NeoTeletext server agrees only CURSOR and SELECT, so it agrees neither TOUCH nor RESUME, whatever a receiver offers. A program receives neither TOUCH events nor a pointer's gone.

3.8 Resuming a session

DOC-1923 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-224, REQ-1335

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-224 Resumption (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-1335 Numbered events, resent after a reconnection (tested, not recorded as met on main).

A WebSocket cannot resume: what was in flight when TCP died is lost silently. When both sides set RESUME, a session outlives its connection, so no key is lost when a connection drops and none reaches the channel twice. Events are numbered implicitly, by the input count both sides keep, and nothing is added to any event.

  1. The token. A channel that sets RESUME in WELCOME gives the session a token of 16 bytes from its cryptographic generator, new in every WELCOME.
  2. The receiver keeps every message of input units it sends, as sent, until an acknowledgement covers it, at most RESEND_BYTES, 16,384 bytes, and 256 messages. A message of no input units is never acknowledged and never kept. When what is kept would pass either bound, the session can no longer be resumed.
  3. The channel holds a session whose connection was lost for at least RESUME_HOLD_MS, 32,000 ms, past the first five retries, with its input count taken. It may drop it sooner under load, and MUST drop it after giving the session to another connection.
  4. On a lost connection the channel releases everything held, so a resumed session starts with nothing held.
  5. A reconnection's first message is HELLO, with RESUME after it: the token and the last acknowledgement taken. In a resumed session HELLO counts nothing.
  6. The channel answers with WELCOME: resumed, with the input count taken, when it holds a session of that token with no connection of its own and the count is at or after the acknowledgement. Otherwise new, and the session begins again from HELLO, nothing of the old resent.
  7. The receiver sends again, before anything else and in the order first sent, every message it keeps that came after the count taken, whole messages only. The count taken is always the end of a message it sent, and one that is not is a protocol error. Then RESIZE, the focus and SELECT, so that the channel sends the selected page again.
  8. The channel drops what it has already taken, so a message that reaches it twice is acted on once.

The count is 16 bits and wraps, compared by RFC 1982 serial arithmetic. The token is a secret of the connection, and goes only on the session's own WebSocket.

Not built. The engine's receiver does all of this (its ResumableSession). The NeoTeletext server does not set RESUME, so a reconnection to it is always a new session, and a RESUME sent to it is ignored.

3.9 Sessions and cookies

DOC-1924 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-495, REQ-1039, REQ-1040, REQ-1471

Cookies are HTTP cookies, RFC 6265bis, held to stricter rules: host-only, scoped to the channel's folder, and sent on the WebSocket upgrade and on each GET. There is no second cookie model. A receiver keeps one cookie jar for a channel, shared by its tabs.

The session cookie. The NeoTeletext server sets one cookie of its own on the 101 response, _session, with Path=/<channel>/, HttpOnly and SameSite=Strict, and Secure on an https origin. Its value is <session>.<expiry>.<signature>: HMAC-SHA-256 of the channel, the session and the expiry, in hexadecimal. It lasts 30 days, renewed at each connection. The server verifies it under its current key and the one before, so a key can be changed. A value that fails is dropped, and a new session begins. Names beginning _ are the server's alone. A program's own cookies are not signed.

A session outlives a connection. A session is a viewer's relationship with a channel's program. It waits 5 minutes after its last connection closes, and the program is then sent closed. Two tabs of one viewer are two connections of one session. A program's state for a session lives in the program: the server is not a database.

At connection, a program is given the cookies the connection opened with, at most 50, without the server's own. They are what the upgrade carried, untrusted, and are not updated while the connection is open.

Mid-connection, a program's cookie message reaches the receiver as a SET_COOKIE record.

Not built. Login is not designed. A program sees sessions only, and no identity. A session is not bound to a device.

3.10 Device-bound sessions

DOC-2032 · reference · informative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-506

Not built. No NeoTeletext server or receiver uses device-bound sessions today. A channel's session is a cookie, and a cookie copied from a viewer's device works for whoever holds it until it expires. The engine holds the means to do better, and nothing turns it on.

What exists. The engine's web crate has a feature, dbsc, which no program built on it enables. It is the client's side of Device Bound Session Credentials, after the W3C Web Application Security Working Group's Editor's Draft of 8 September 2026: where a port can keep a signing key that cannot leave the device, a server issues short-lived cookies only after the client proves it holds the key, so a copied cookie soon becomes useless. The agent never sees a private key: a port gives it keys through UserAgent::set_device_keys. A port with no such storage does not offer it, and its sessions work as any cookie does.

  • It works over HTTPS only, and with ES256 only.
  • A session registers at the responding origin's own endpoint, and its scope and refresh address are on that origin. A session for a whole site is refused, so a session covers its own host alone.
  • A session is refreshed before any request in its scope that would go without one of its cookies, matched by name, and again with the server's challenge after a 403.
  • Sessions are held in memory, at most four, and an origin has the agent register at most four times an hour.
  • A session identifier is refused unless it is non-empty and every byte is 0x21 to 0x7E, since it is written back into Sec-Secure-Session-Id. Instructions that name a member twice are refused.

What the server does not do. The NeoTeletext server sends no registration, so a receiver that had the feature would never be asked to use it. Its _session cookie is a plain signed cookie.

3.11 What is legal when

DOC-1925 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-205, REQ-1481, REQ-1555

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1481 Every selection has an ending: when a program does not answer in time, or is not running, the viewer is told the channel is unavailable and the connection stays open (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

In this stateA receiver may sendA channel may send
Upgrade answered, no HELLOHELLO, first and onceNothing a receiver must act on: pages wait for the program's answer, which waits for SELECT or the first selection's bound
HELLO sent, no WELCOMENothingWELCOME, or a close of 4001. A receiver waits at most 10 seconds.
Welcomed, no selectionRESIZE, FOCUS_IN or FOCUS_OUT, SELECT. A resumed session's messages go first. No stamped input, which has no page on screen.Index, cursor and control records, and nothing of a page's content until the program answers
A selection awaiting its endingAnything, a new SELECT included, which replaces it, and stamped input on the page still on screen, dropped if it no longer matchesThe selection's page, or DENIED, and records. A later change to an older selection is dropped.
A selection ended with a pageEverything, while keys goThat page's changes, records and a REDIRECT
A selection ended with DENIEDSELECT, and nothing on a page, as none is on screenA page for that selection, if the program recovers
After a closeNothing on that connectionNothing. A new connection is a new session.

Anything else on the receiver's side is a protocol error, or dropped when the stamp says so. A stamped event sent before any selection is dropped and counted by the NeoTeletext server, which does not close the connection for it.

Not built. The NeoTeletext server does not yet hold a receiver to this order. It answers a second HELLO with a second WELCOME, and closes nothing for raw text, RESIZE or another event before HELLO, nor for a SELECT, or a MOUSE of a device other than the mouse, whose capability was not agreed. A receiver keeps to the order all the same, and another server may close.

3.12 What overload does

DOC-1926 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-208, REQ-1356, REQ-1462, REQ-1557

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1356 Never behind the network (tested, not recorded as met on main); REQ-1462 Only packets of the allowed kinds downstream; a receiver discards, counts and reports the rest (tested, not recorded as met on main).

Each message type has one rule for when it cannot keep up, and nothing is merged or dropped beyond them.

MessageThe rule
A key, a button, a contact's down and up, PASTENever dropped by the channel, and limited to 120 input units a second by the receiver, which never drops a release to keep to it
A moveThe latest of a contact's replaces the unsent one, at most 60 a second, and it waits while the socket has 1,024 bytes or more unsent
The wheelUnsent deltas are summed
HOVERGoes as a control is crossed, and counts nothing
COMPOSEHeld to the rate as one unit
SELECTA later one replaces an earlier, whose answer is dropped. The engine's receiver does not hold it to the rate: it counts no units, and the rate counts units.
A page frame, a record, a sound or raw packetsAn answer to a replaced selection is dropped by its number. A connection's queue on the server holds 256 messages of every kind, and what will not fit is dropped, the newest first, so a receiver that far behind can miss a change until the next whole frame.
LOGA receiver passes on at most 16 for each page frame it takes, and 16 before the first
Lines to a programAt most 4,096 lines wait for a program. The newest beyond that are dropped, and the server's log says so.

An invalid packet costs a count and no more. A receiver reports the count at most once in 20 seconds, and the server reports input dropped for its stamp at the same pace.

Not built. A policy for each source: the oldest whole record dropped for live audio, the latest of an audio command kept, and a close for raw packets that must not lose data. Nothing ever drops part of a page frame, but a slow connection loses the newest sound as it loses anything else.

3.13 The required core, and what is not understood

DOC-1927 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-200, REQ-201, REQ-1476

A receiver that does this much works on every channel: the upgrade with its subprotocol; HELLO, and WELCOME; SELECT, having set the SELECT capability; a page's packets; and stopping or trying again on a close. It need read no record but WELCOME to show a page, and DENIED to say why none came.

A channel's required core is: WELCOME; a page frame or DENIED for every SELECT; and closing with the codes of this part.

Everything else is optional, and each kind of thing has one rule for what is not understood.

WhatA reader of one it does not know
A core event, 0x01 to 0x3FA protocol error: it has no length to step over
An extension event, 0x40 to 0x7FSkipped by its length
Type 0x00, or 0x80 to 0xFFA protocol error
A flag bit of an eventA protocol error
A capabilityIgnored, and not agreed in WELCOME
A record type on data channel 12Skipped by the type byte, not counted
A control messageSkipped by its length, or ignored when withdrawn
A field or a type of a program's messageRefused whole, with a refused message: the program protocol is exact, as its two ends are released together
A member of channel.ttxThe file is refused

3.14 Decided, not built: the socket options of the path

DOC-2127 · reference · informative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-1349

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1349 Every socket on the input path sets TCP_NODELAY, the TLS terminator in front of the server included, and the server's sockets set TCP_THIN_LINEAR_TIMEOUTS on Linux (not implemented on main).

Not built in full. Every socket on the path of an input event is to have TCP_NODELAY, whatever terminates TLS in front of the server included, and the server's own sockets are to have TCP_THIN_LINEAR_TIMEOUTS on Linux. Nagle's algorithm would otherwise hold a small write until an earlier one is acknowledged, and a lone lost segment would wait for a doubling retransmission timeout.

Today the server sets TCP_NODELAY on each viewer's socket and the receivers set it on theirs. The server does not set TCP_THIN_LINEAR_TIMEOUTS, and the terminator in front of a deployed server has not been checked.

4 The upstream protocol

DOC-1781 · section

4.1 A terminal on a line to a host

DOC-1928 · concept · informative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-200, REQ-1332

A channel that takes input is a host, and the receiver is its terminal. The viewer types, each key goes to the channel at once, and the channel decides what it means and answers by sending the rows that changed. The receiver decides only whether a key goes, and the channel only what it means. Nothing ever runs in the receiver: a game, a form, a chat and a shell are the same to it. A channel that feeds an ordinary terminal program translates events into whatever encoding that program asked for.

Each binary message a receiver sends is a piece of one ordered input stream, the concatenation of all binary messages on that connection. Four rules shape it.

  • One message per event, never held back. Nothing waits on a timer but the pointer's moves.
  • As few bytes as possible. Raw text is one byte for an ASCII character, and the commonest events take a few bytes.
  • No guessing. An event starts with 0xFF, which never occurs in UTF-8, so the start of every event is known with no timeout, and its length from its type.
  • Bounded everywhere. Every length has a maximum, and every reader refuses whole what breaks a bound.

The stream is defined so that it also works over any reliable ordered byte stream, such as a microcontroller's serial line. Everything in it is fixed-size or length-prefixed, and can be decoded byte by byte with a few dozen bytes of state.

4.2 Framing and encoding

DOC-1929 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-200, REQ-201

A message MUST hold whole events and whole UTF-8 characters, so that a channel can read each message alone. Text may be split between messages anywhere between characters. A message is at most MAX_MESSAGE, 8,192 bytes.

NameEncoding
u8One byte
uvUnsigned varint: 7 bits a byte, least significant group first, the high bit set on every byte but the last. 0 to 127 take one byte. At most 5 bytes, and a longer or non-minimal encoding is a protocol error.
svSigned varint: zigzag, (n << 1) ^ (n >> 31), then uv. -64 to 63 take one byte.
struv byte length, then that many bytes of UTF-8, within the field's own maximum.

A field marked ? in this part is present only when its flag bit is set.

A protocol error closes the connection with code 4000. What was read before the close may still be acted on, a message behind the faulty one included. A field over its maximum, a message over its bound and a varint over 5 bytes or not minimal are each one.

4.3 Raw text

DOC-1930 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-200, REQ-201

Any byte other than 0xFF begins raw UTF-8 text. Raw text is what does not come from a key: an input method's committed text, and a phone's. A key is never sent as raw text: every key is a KEY event carrying its text.

  • Text MUST NOT contain control characters, U+0000 to U+001F and U+007F to U+009F. Enter, Tab, Backspace, Escape and every Control, Alt, Super, Hyper or Meta combination are key events.
  • Bytes 0xC0, 0xC1 and 0xF5 to 0xFE outside an event, and malformed UTF-8, are protocol errors.
  • A character cut short by the next event, as in E2 82 FF 05, is malformed at once.
  • On a WebSocket a character cut by the end of its message is a protocol error, because a message is read alone. On a byte stream, a character cut by the end of what has arrived waits for the rest.
  • Raw text carries no stamp: a channel takes it for the selection as it stands.

Why a reader cannot lose its place. No UTF-8 sequence holds 0xFF, so text can never forge an event. Inside an event, position decides: 0xFF may be a field's byte, and is read as one because every field's length is known from what came before. An extension's payload is taken whole by its length. A flag bit, type or value nobody defined is a protocol error, not something to step over, so there is no resynchronising, and no state in which a reader carries on having lost its place.

4.4 The events

DOC-1931 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-202, REQ-1556

An event is 0xFF, a type byte, then its fields.

Type rangeLayout
0x01 to 0x3FCore events, of fixed layout. An unknown core type is a protocol error.
0x40 to 0x7FExtensions: a uv length, then the payload, at most 8,192 bytes. A reader skips one it does not know. A field that a known extension's payload cuts short is a protocol error, and so are bytes after its last field.
0x00, 0x80 to 0xFFReserved: a protocol error.
TypeNameFieldsWhen
0x01KEYThe stamp, flags, key and moreEvery press, repeat and release
0x02MOUSEThe stamp, flags u8, button u8, col uv, row uv, px? uv, py? uv, mods? u8Every press and release over the page, and every move that changes the dot
0x03WHEELThe stamp, flags u8, dx sv, dy sv, col uv, row uv, px? uv, py? uv, mods? u8While the pointer is over the page
0x04PASTEThe stamp, str of at most 4,000 bytesWhile the page has the keyboard focus
0x05FOCUS_INNoneAlways
0x06FOCUS_OUTNoneAlways
0x07RESIZEcols uv, rows uv, pw uv, ph uvAlways
0x08COMPOSEThe stamp, cursor uv, str of at most 256 bytesWhile an input method composes
0x09HELLOversion uv, caps uv, agent? strFirst, once
0x0ANoneWas PAGE until 31 October 2026. It is unused, so it is a protocol error like any unknown core type.Never
0x40SELECTLength, then the place as textWhenever the connection's selection changes, and at the start of a session
0x41, 0x42NoneReserved. They were TUNE and UNTUNE, never built.Never
0x43HOVERLength, the stamp, flags u8, col uv, row uvAs the pointer enters and leaves a control
0x44TOUCHLength, the stamp, flags u8, id u8, col uv, row uv, px? uv, py? uvEvery contact after the first, when both sides set TOUCH
0x45RESUMELength, token 16 bytes, acked u16 big-endian, exactly 18 bytes of payloadAfter HELLO, in a reconnection's first message

There are no modes. Every event goes to the channel whenever keys go to it, and the channel ignores what it does not use. A receiver MUST NOT send TOUCH, a MOUSE of a device other than the mouse, RESUME or SELECT unless both sides set the capability it needs.

A viewer's sending rules, such as when keys go at all, are what a receiver chooses. The rules for what a channel learns are in where the pointer is.

4.5 HELLO

DOC-1932 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-205, REQ-520

HELLO is the first event of a session, and is sent once. version is 7 and caps is the receiver's capabilities. A value of caps with bit 16 set is a protocol error.

agent is the receiver's user agent string, NeoTeletext/0.1 (macOS), or product/version (platform) neoteletext/version for another program on the engine: at most 64 bytes of printable ASCII. It is present when caps has AGENT, and is sent only over a transport without HTTP, such as a serial line. Over the WebSocket the channel has it from the upgrade's User-Agent header, so it is not sent twice.

The stream's first bytes are therefore always FF 09, and a channel answers with WELCOME or a close of 4001.

4.6 Selected, active and focused

DOC-1933 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-1553, REQ-1554

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1554 Input reaches the selected page only while it is focused: keys while the page holds the keyboard focus, pointer events while the pointer is over the page (to be rechecked on main).

Three words are defined once, here.

Selected
The page and sub-page a tab shows, named by SELECT. A connection has at most one selection, which is what the channel sends, keeps and compares.
Active
What a selected page is to the program. An active page receives the program's updates, whatever the viewer's input is doing. Only a new selection, a SELECT of nothing included, ends them.
Focused
What a selected page is for the viewer's input, and only for input. A page has the keyboard focus while keys go to it, which FOCUS_IN and FOCUS_OUT tell the channel, and the pointer focus while the pointer or a touch is over its page area, which MOUSE gone ends.

So a page can be active without being focused: in a tab behind another, in a window behind another application, while the viewer types an address, or with the pointer outside it. Losing either focus MUST NOT change the selection or stop updates.

4.7 The stamp

DOC-1934 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-1555, REQ-1557

KEY, PASTE, COMPOSE, MOUSE, WHEEL, TOUCH and HOVER each begin with page uv and sub uv, before their other fields: the page and sub-code on screen in the tab when the event happened. That is what the viewer was looking at, not merely what was selected: between a new selection and the arrival of its page, the old page is still on screen, and a click aimed at it carries the old page.

  • page is 0x100 to 0x8FF. sub is a sub-code 0x0000 to 0x3F7E, with no bit outside the thirteen, and never 0x3F7F, because a page on screen is always a particular sub-page. Anything else is a protocol error, and so is a page of 0.
  • Page 100, sub-code 0000 is the three bytes 80 02 00, and a stamp is four bytes at most.
  • No other event has one. FOCUS_IN and FOCUS_OUT are always acted on, since FOCUS_OUT releases held keys and must never be dropped.

The channel's check. A channel compares each stamp with the connection's selection as last recorded. When the page and sub-code are the same, the event is acted on. When they differ, the event is dropped and nothing of it applied, the drop is counted, at most one log line an interval names the count, and the connection is kept.

A release is never dropped: one whose stamp does not match is still applied, as a release of what the connection holds, so that nothing stays held. So is a pointer's gone. A press with a stamp that does not match is dropped.

The selection stays the only truth: a stamp can only cause a drop, never send input to a page other than the selection.

4.8 Selecting a page

DOC-1753 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-1090, REQ-1388, REQ-1502, REQ-1693

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1388 The page and sub-page a connection has selected are sent with SELECT, which carries no view number (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-1693 Every channel has one complete, fixed, defined URL map: a channel is a folder, and a place in it is the end of the path (to be rechecked on main).

SELECT is the only message that names a selection. It is an extension, 0x40, whose payload is the place as text, the same text the URL map reads.

PayloadSelects
302The page: sub-code 3F7F, "no particular page sub-code", for a page keyed by its number alone, or a link or bookmark that names no sub-page
302/0002One sub-page: a sub-code 0000 to 3F7E
Either followed by ? and a queryThe same, with the query of the address selected, as written: name=value pairs joined by &, in bytes 0x21 to 0x7E with no #, at most 8,000 bytes
EmptyNothing: the tab no longer shows a page of the channel. As a connection's first selection it counts as none, and the connection is shown the channel's initial page, which is then its selection.

Digits are capitals, in the one spelling of the map. Anything else is a protocol error, as is a query with no page, a ? with nothing after it, and a sub-code of 3F7F. A query belongs to this one selection: a SELECT that does not carry it leaves it behind.

When it goes. A receiver MUST send SELECT whenever the page or sub-page shown in its tab changes, on that tab's own connection, whatever the focus: a page keyed or followed by a link, a sub-page stepped to or chosen from the list. It sends it at the start of each session, after HELLO and after any RESUME, when the tab shows a page of the channel, so a reconnection is sent its page again. It sends an empty SELECT when the tab leaves the channel's pages. It sends one for a page a REDIRECT moved it to. It is sent only when both sides set SELECT, and a receiver that does not set it has no selection and is sent no page of its own choosing.

SELECT counts no input units. A SELECT that repeats a selection that stands, awaited or shown, the same page, sub-code and query, is no new selection: a server does nothing for it and sends no answer. A page selected with 3F7F becomes a sub-page when it is answered, so once page 100 has come as 100/0000, a second SELECT of 100 is a new selection, and only 100/0000 repeats it. A selection DENIED ended no longer stands, so a SELECT that repeats it is a new selection: the server asks its program again, and it has an ending of its own.

What the tab is then sent. Every sub-code a page has is one of its sub-pages, all of them peers, and the page's order is ascending sub-code, 0000 first when present. For a page selection, the tab shows the page's first sub-page in that order, and that sub-page then is the selection, exactly as if the viewer had chosen it: the channel answers with it, the receiver sends no second SELECT, and stepping goes on from it. For a sub-page selection, the tab shows that sub-page alone, and is sent only it and its changes, while the page's other sub-pages change, until it selects something else.

Stepping to the next or previous sub-page is a selection of that sub-code. Sub-pages are never sent with their page, and nothing rotates on a live channel: the sub-page shown changes only when the viewer selects another, or when the program moves the tab, which is itself a selection.

Its ending. Every selection ends with a page or with DENIED, by the deadline.

Rests on: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 9.6.1 Packets X/27/0 to X/27/3 for Editorial Linking; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause A.1 Page numbering

4.9 KEY

DOC-1935 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-203, REQ-204, REQ-1350

The stamp, flags u8, key uv, mods? u8, text? str of at most 32 bytes, base? uv.

flags bitMeaning
0 and 1Action: 0 press, 1 repeat, 2 release. 3 is a protocol error.
2F: key is a functional code, not a code point. A code that names no key is a protocol error.
3M: mods present. It is omitted when 0, and present and 0 is a protocol error.
4T: text present: the key produced text.
5B: base present: it differs from key.
6L: lost, on a release only: the key did not come up, because the page lost the input. A protocol error with any other action.
7Reserved: a protocol error when set.
  • key: for a character key, the Unicode code point of the key without Shift, so Control-Shift-A sends a with Shift and Control; otherwise a functional code.
  • mods: 1 Shift, 2 Alt (Option on macOS), 4 Control, 8 Super (Command, Windows), 16 Hyper, 32 Meta, 64 Caps Lock, 128 Num Lock, as the Kitty keyboard protocol has them.
  • text: the text the press produced, if any, with no control character.
  • base: the code point the same physical key has in a United States layout, so a channel's shortcuts work on any layout.

A receiver sends every key: every press, repeat and release, the keys that only modify or lock included, and the dead key. A channel that only types takes text from presses and repeats. A release is sent as FF 01, the stamp, its flags and key.

4.10 Functional key codes

DOC-1936 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-204

Every key that is not a character has one code, which never changes: new keys are only ever added. Codes 1 to 127 take one byte.
