4.11 International keyboards

DOC-1937 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-215

The protocol carries what the viewer typed, in whatever language and on whatever layout, and leaves its meaning to the channel.

  1. Text is Unicode, after the layout. A key's result is sent as the characters the system's layout produced. A receiver MUST NOT translate text into a teletext character set: the channel maps it onto the sets of the page it draws.
  2. Logical order. Text goes in the order it was typed, which for right-to-left scripts is logical order, never the visual order a page may be drawn in.
  3. Normalized. Text is sent in Unicode Normalization Form C, so that a channel compares what it receives without normalizing it again.
  4. Dead keys. A dead key sends no text: it sends KEY with the functional code of Dead and base. The key after it sends the composed character as text, or both characters when the two do not compose.
  5. AltGr is a level shift, not a modifier. A character typed with AltGr is sent as KEY with that text and no modifiers. A receiver on a system that reports AltGr as Control and Alt held together MUST NOT report it so. AltGr itself is the key AltGraph.
  6. Input methods compose in the receiver: their preedit goes as COMPOSE, and what they commit as raw text. Their own keys have functional codes from 128.
  7. key follows the layout, and base the position. A channel binds a letter by key, and a position, such as a game's movement keys, by base.
  8. Digits. The keypad's keys are their functional codes, with their characters as text. A layout that types native digits sends those characters, and a channel that selects pages by number SHOULD take the decimal digits of any script as the digits 0 to 9.
  9. Lock states and layout changes send nothing of their own beyond the lock bits in mods, so the viewer's choice of language is never announced.
  10. Phones. A phone keyboard's text is text under the same rules, with no modifiers.

4.12 MOUSE and WHEEL

DOC-1938 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-202, REQ-221, REQ-222, REQ-1351, REQ-1369

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-222 Gone (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

flags bitsMOUSEWHEEL
0 and 1Action: 0 press, 1 release, 2 move, 3 goneUnused, and a protocol error when set
2px, py presentThe same
3mods presentThe same
4 and 5The device: 0 a mouse or trackpad, 1 touch, 2 a pen, 3 a protocol error. Set only when both sides set TOUCH, and otherwise 0.A protocol error when set
6 and 7A protocol error when setA protocol error when set
  • button: on press and release, 0 left, 1 middle, 2 right, 3 back, 4 forward, 5 and up others. On move and gone, a bit mask of the buttons held.
  • col, row: the character cell, from the top left of the page: columns 0 to 39, rows 0 to 24. A value beyond them is a protocol error.
  • px, py: the dot of the page image from the same origin, 12 to a cell across and 20 down, sent whenever the receiver knows them.
  • dx, dy: scroll in 1/120ths of a line, positive right and down. A wheel that moves in notches sends at least a whole line, 120, a notch. A trackpad's continuous scrolling is sent as it comes.

Gone says the pointer has gone and nothing it held was pressed. It goes when the pointer leaves the open page or keys stop going to the channel, after the waiting move, and when the system or the receiver cancels a contact. button is the mask held, and col and row the last position sent. What it held is released without a click, and a channel MUST NOT act on it as on a release.

A link the page declares is the receiver's on every page: a click or tap on it follows it, and no press, release or contact on it goes to the channel. A drag begun off a link stays the channel's when it crosses one.

4.13 TOUCH and pens

DOC-1939 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-223, REQ-1362, REQ-1363, REQ-1364, REQ-1371

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-223 Touch (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-1371 A pen (tested, not recorded as met on main).

TOUCH is an extension, 0x44, sent only when both sides set TOUCH. Its payload is the stamp, flags u8, id u8, col uv, row uv, px? uv, py? uv, and nothing after.

  • flags bits 0 and 1 are the action, as MOUSE's: 0 down, 1 up, 2 move, 3 gone. Bit 2 says px and py are present. Bits 3 to 7 are a protocol error.
  • id is 1 to 9, the contact's number from its beginning to its end, the lowest free when it begins. 0 or above 9 is a protocol error. At most ten contacts are on the page at once.
  • The first contact down, while no other is down and no mouse button is held, goes as MOUSE: a press of button 0, moves with button 0 held, a release as it lifts, device 1. Every further contact goes as TOUCH. An eleventh sends nothing until one ends. Without the capability, only the first goes, as the mouse.
  • A contact's beginning and end are written the moment the system reports them, never held to recognise a gesture. A cancelled contact goes as gone. No move goes for a finger before it is down or after it lifts.
  • A pen goes as MOUSE with device 2: touching is button 0, its barrel button button 2, and hovering, where the hardware reports it, moves with no button. Pressure, tilt and a contact's size are not sent.

While keys go to the channel, every contact on its page is the channel's, two fingers and a pinch included. The system's gestures from the screen's edges are the system's.

Not built. The NeoTeletext server does not set TOUCH, and passes a program no contact after the first.

4.14 HOVER

DOC-1940 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-220, REQ-1361

HOVER is an extension, 0x43. Its payload is the stamp, flags u8, col uv and row uv. Bit 0 of flags is 1 when the pointer left the control and 0 when it entered, and any other bit is a protocol error. col and row are the control's first cell, its top left.

It goes as the pointer enters and leaves a control of the open page while keys go to the channel. Leaving one control for another sends the leave, then the enter. It lets a channel show something as the pointer passes, a row's summary or a help line, without working out from the moves which control the pointer is over. It goes only for a mouse or a pen that hovers, never for a finger.

4.15 PASTE and COMPOSE

DOC-1941 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-202, REQ-1384

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1384 Mouse, paste and composition (NeoTeletext) (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

PASTE is the whole paste as one event: the stamp, then a str of at most 4,000 bytes. It may contain control characters: a channel treats it as data, never as keystrokes. A longer paste is cut at a character boundary.

COMPOSE is an input method's preedit, as it changes: the stamp, cursor uv, a code-point offset into the text, at most its length, then a str of at most 256 bytes with no control character. An empty string ends composition.

4.16 FOCUS_IN, FOCUS_OUT and RESIZE

DOC-1942 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-202, REQ-1643

FOCUS_IN and FOCUS_OUT tell a channel when keys start and stop going to the page. They carry nothing, and are always acted on, though a program hears focus and blur only after the connection's first selection. FOCUS_OUT releases everything held. A receiver sends one of them after WELCOME, and again whenever keys start or stop going.

RESIZE gives the page's text area in cells and the page image in pixels as the receiver draws it. A teletext page is always 40 by 25, and cols or rows of 0 or above that is a protocol error, so RESIZE changes only when the drawn size does. pw and ph are 0 when unknown. The NeoTeletext browser sends 0, and the server passes no RESIZE to a program.

4.17 The input count and the rate

DOC-1943 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-208, REQ-209, REQ-225, REQ-1334

The input count is how much input of the session the channel has acted on, and is what a cursor record acknowledges. Each event counts one, HELLO and RESIZE and FOCUS among them, each character of raw text counts one, and a PASTE counts one. What is never acknowledged counts none: a move and gone, MOUSE's and TOUCH's, HOVER, COMPOSE, RESUME and SELECT. A receiver counts what it sends the same way. In a resumed session HELLO counts nothing.

The count wraps at 65,536 and is compared by RFC 1982 serial arithmetic: an older acknowledgement changes nothing, and one beyond what was sent is ignored.

A message of no input units is never acknowledged. A receiver takes a cursor record wherever it comes. The NeoTeletext server ends every page frame with one, which carries the count, and sends one alone only when no frame has carried the count ACKNOWLEDGEMENT_WAIT_MS, 250 ms, after the first input it covers, and never before a selection is made. So an answered key costs one cursor record, or two when the answer comes after the wait, and a run of keys on a page that publishes nothing costs one for each wait. A receiver waits for none of this before it shows anything. It counts every item it reads, a stamp's drop included, so the count measures what it took and not what a program did with it.

The rate. A receiver sends at most MAX_PER_SECOND, 120, input units in any second, counted over a sliding second, and MUST NOT drop a release, a key's, a button's or a contact's up, to keep to it. COMPOSE counts nothing in the input count, but is held to the rate as one unit, since nothing else bounds it. A move, gone and HOVER are not held to it, so the pointer can never cost a key.

4.18 Held input

DOC-1944 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-207

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-207 Held input is released on focus loss, pointer leaving, selection change and connection loss, each release marked as lost input, never deselecting the page (to be rechecked on livedevelopment).

A key or button is held when it went down and has not come up. It is released whenever the page loses the input, and releasing never deselects the page.

SignalReleases
FOCUS_OUTEvery held key, button and contact
A pointer's goneThe buttons and the contact it held, without a click
A change of selection, an empty SELECT or the program moving the tabEverything
A connection closing or dropping, or a session ending without resumptionEverything

A receiver. Before the FOCUS_OUT or SELECT that ends the input, a receiver that knows what is held sends a KEY release for each held key with the L flag set, stamped with the page that held it, and gone for the pointer and each contact.

A channel keeps what each connection holds. On a signal it treats whatever is still held as released itself, and acts on nothing else, because a receiver may have sent nothing: a dropped connection cannot, and a terminal that reports no releases never does.

The mark. Every such release reaches a channel's program marked lost: true, so a game can tell "the player let go" from "the player left". A real key-up or button-up carries no mark.

4.19 The pointer's moves

DOC-1945 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-221, REQ-1352, REQ-1353, REQ-1354, REQ-1356, REQ-1366

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1352 Sixty a second (tested, not recorded as met on main); REQ-1356 Never behind the network (tested, not recorded as met on main); REQ-1366 Moves per contact (tested, not recorded as met on main).

A receiver sends every move that changes the dot under the pointer while it is over the open page, buttons held or not, within these rules, which keep the pointer from ever delaying a key.

  • Sixty a second. At most one move in each period of 1000/60 ms. While moves follow each other, the kth period ends at the time of the move that began the run plus k times 1,000,000 / 60 microseconds, counted exactly, so that the average is exactly 60 a second with no drift.
  • At once if it can, the latest kept. A move that comes 1000/60 ms or more after the last goes at once, and begins a run. One that comes sooner waits in one slot, replacing any position waiting there. An unchanged position does not go again, and nothing runs while the pointer is still.
  • Order. Before any other event is written, the move waiting in the slot is written first, so a release never comes before the move that ended the drag.
  • Never behind the network. A move is written only while the socket's unsent bytes are under 1,024. Otherwise it waits in one slot a contact, and newer moves replace it. Any other event writes the held move first, then itself, and never waits behind a move.
  • Each contact has a slot and a pace of its own, and the moves of several contacts waiting at one moment are written together.

4.20 Where the pointer is, at any size

DOC-1946 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-1359, REQ-1377

Verified by inspection. REQ-1359 Privacy stated: Read on 2 November 2026: DOC-1946's paragraph on what a channel learns and DOC-2033's row on the channel tracking the pointer, and its Privacy paragraph, say that a channel learns the pointer's position over its own open page while keys go to it, and nothing elsewhere: not over another tab, the receiver's own controls or another window. No test can check this, because the requirement is that the document says it.

A receiver draws a page at whatever size its screen allows, and every position it sends is in the page's own units, so that a channel answers the pointer the same way at every size.

  • The units. The image is 480 by 500 dots, 12 to a cell across and 20 down, from the page's top left. The dot is always inside the cell: px / 12 = col and py / 20 = row, rounded down.
  • The conversion. A receiver takes the pointer's position, subtracts the top left of the rectangle where the image was last drawn, and scales by the image's size in dots over the rectangle's size, rounding down: px = (x - left) × 480 / width, and the same down. The border of the screen colour around the image is not the page. A contact's position is the point the system reports for it, converted the same way.
  • Only the open page, at rest. Positions go only for the page keys go to, while it is drawn where the receiver will keep it: not over a page still zooming, sliding or shown as one of many.
  • Drawn larger, several screen pixels show each dot. A move within one dot changes nothing and is not sent, so a page drawn larger costs no more moves. Drawn smaller, one screen pixel covers more than one dot, so some dots cannot be pointed at and a move may step several dots at once. The cell stays exact until a cell is smaller than a pixel. At a size that is not a whole multiple of the image, the dot sent may be the neighbour of the one drawn under the pointer's tip, and is never another cell's.
  • A channel tests where a click landed by its cell, and treats the dot as finer detail, exact only at the image's own size or larger. A channel MUST cope with no dot at all: a receiver that knows only cells sends none.
  • Double height and double size. The cell sent is the grid cell under the pointer, not the cell where a double-height character begins. A channel whose control spans two rows answers both.
  • A trusted check. A receiver SHOULD refuse a cell outside the page, and a dot outside the image or its cell, before anything is encoded.

What a channel learns. It learns the pointer's position over its own open page, its clicks, drags and moves, and the contacts on it, while keys go to it. It learns nothing elsewhere: not over another tab, the receiver's own controls or another window, and nothing while keys do not go to it.

4.21 Controls and links under the pointer

DOC-1947 · concept · informative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-218

A page says which of its cells it answers itself, in its own packets, so that a receiver can show them as it shows links. While keys go to the open page's channel, the pointer is a hand over them, and a control or a link under the pointer takes a look the receiver draws from the page's own colours, with what Level 2.5 already does to them.

  • Hover: each colour of its cells becomes the same entry of the adjacent colour table, the pairing of the Additional Flash Functions' colour flash: CLUT 0 with 1, 2 with 3. A page chooses its hover colours by defining CLUTs 2 and 3 in packet X/28/0 Format 1 and drawing its controls from them. With Table 30's colours, hover is the half-intensity colour of CLUT 1.
  • Pressed, while a button is held on it or a contact is on it: the hover colours with foreground and background exchanged, as Invert Colour exchanges them. A finger gives no hover look.

The receiver draws both itself, at once, and sends nothing for them, so a page lights under the pointer without the channel drawing a copy of it for each viewer. While keys do not go to the channel a page's controls are not shown, since a click on them would not reach it.

A control is only where a click lands: the click still goes to the channel as MOUSE, at its cell, as every click over the page does, and the channel decides what it means. Declaring controls asks for nothing.

4.22 The phone keyboard

DOC-2029 · reference · informative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-98, REQ-1387

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-98 A phone as a channel's keyboard (not implemented on livedevelopment).

A phone can be the keyboard of a channel. It scans a QR code and sends its keys through the relay of qrkeyboard, another project (qrkeyboard.com), whose protocol 3 a NeoTeletext receiver speaks as the screen. That protocol is qrkeyboard's, and is defined there. This item says what a NeoTeletext receiver and neottx keyboard do with it. A channel need do nothing: what reaches it is ordinary input, under the rules of international keyboards.

What reaches a channel. A phone is a keyboard and no more: its keys carry no modifiers.

From the phoneBecomes
A key of the layout going down, named as W3C KeyboardEvent.key names itA KEY press. A single printable character is that character, in lower case, with itself as text. Any other name is its functional code with no text, so the colour keys are 92 to 95, and a name with no code is Unidentified.
That key coming upA KEY release. Only a key the screen holds is released, and a key held past 15 seconds is released without its up.
Text typed in the phone's boxRaw text, with control characters taken out, in messages of whole characters
A special key of the box: Backspace, Tab, Enter, Escape or an arrow, with a countA press and a release of that key, as many times as the count, at most 10,000

neottx keyboard bridges a phone to a channel that takes input, so that a channel gets a phone keyboard without implementing qrkeyboard: neottx keyboard <ws address> [--relay <address>] [--pin] [--name <name>] [--qr-page <mpp> --qr-output <file>]. It offers a text box, a direction pad and the four colour keys.

  • It connects as a receiver that draws nothing: HELLO with no capabilities, so no SELECT, and after WELCOME a RESIZE of 40 by 25 with its pixels unknown, and FOCUS_IN.
  • With no SELECT capability the connection takes the channel's initial page, and a page the channel sends is its selection.
  • It reads the header of each page the channel sends, and stamps the phone's keys with that page and sub-code. It sends none before the first, since a key needs a page to be stamped with.
  • It writes the QR code to the terminal with the warning that anyone who can see it can type, and with --qr-page as a page's mosaics too. neottx qr does that for any text.
  • A dropped phone may come back for 30 seconds and keep the screen.

The screen's side, which the engine's crate neoteletext-qrkeyboard gives neottx and the browser:

  • It asks the relay for an instance, and shows a QR code of <relay>/app#c=<code>&k=<key>&h=<hash>. The join code, the pairing key when there is no PIN, and the configuration's hash go only in the address's fragment, which a phone never sends. The hash is SHA-256 of the configuration in qrkeyboard's canonical JSON, cut to 128 bits.
  • It pairs by a fresh salt from each end and, with a PIN, SPAKE2 (RFC 9382) on P-256, bound to the code and the hash. Each frame is then sealed with AES-256-GCM under a counter nonce, one key each way, and each counter is taken once and in order.
  • Without a PIN, a phone counts as connected at its first sealed frame that opens, not at its salt, which anyone on the relay can send.
  • A PIN is four digits, and five failed runs lock the screen.
  • A relay's WebSocket on another host than the relay is refused. Frames are at most 1,024 bytes, and an HTTP response's body at most 64 KiB. Keys outside the layout are ignored, and everything the relay and the phone send is untrusted.

In the NeoTeletext browser, a phone's keys go from a keyboard process of their own, which alone holds the pairing secret, to the trusted core and on to the interface, which acts on them as on its own: into a page, or choosing pages. They reach the address field only after the viewer puts the caret there with the pointer. One phone at a time. Finish in the strip ends it, and a dropped phone holds the screen for at most 30 seconds. The QR code shows only from the panel, with its warning: whoever scans it has the keyboard.

What the relay learns. It carries sealed frames, so a passive relay or anyone on the network learns a phone keyboard's name, layout, addresses, sizes and timing, and not its keys. The relay's operator also serves the phone its page, so that operator, or whoever compromises the relay's server, can read the PIN and the keys.

4.23 Security and privacy of input

DOC-2033 · concept · informative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-1039, REQ-1359, REQ-1378

Verified by inspection. REQ-1359 Privacy stated: Read on 2 November 2026: DOC-1946's paragraph on what a channel learns and DOC-2033's row on the channel tracking the pointer, and its Privacy paragraph, say that a channel learns the pointer's position over its own open page while keys go to it, and nothing elsewhere: not over another tab, the receiver's own controls or another window. No test can check this, because the requirement is that the document says it.

What is protected is the viewer's keystrokes and reading habits, the truth of the screen and of a receiver's own controls, a receiver's readers, its own keys, and a phone's pairing secret. The attackers considered are a channel's author, a network attacker, a compromised part of a receiver, a hostile relay, anyone who can see the screen, and a stranger who scanned a QR code. A receiver is a terminal: no page can run code, and nothing on the input path can make a receiver fetch, leave the channel, or do anything but draw.

ThreatAnswer
A page or a channel starts taking keys unaskedOnly channel.ttx declares input, and a page cannot. A receiver says where keys go, and they go only to the open page of the active tab.
Keys go somewhere elseThe connection's address comes from the channel's own address, and nothing in the protocol names another.
A keylogger on a plain connectionA receiver says "Not secure" on ws://. wss is the default for any channel worth typing into.
A web page reaching a channel with a viewer's cookiesAn upgrade that carries Origin is refused, so a receiver cannot be written in a web page.
A compression oracle on private rowsNo permessage-deflate.
A server told apart from a receiver by what it calls itselfNo server reads a receiver's agent text. Capabilities are the only signal.
The channel tracks the pointerIt learns the pointer over its own open page while keys go to it, and nothing elsewhere.
Fingerprinting by keyboard layoutbase names a key's position, not a layout, and lock states go only in mods. There is no clock: a channel learns typing rhythm only from arrival times, which the network blurs, and no physical key code.
A paste runs as keystrokesPASTE is one event of known length, which a channel treats as data.
A flood from a channelPushed rows are drawn at the display's rate, records are bounded and checked, and a protocol error closes the connection.
A flood from a receiver120 input units a second at the phone relay, the keyboard process and the receiver's trusted part, and moves at most 60 a second a contact, counted nothing, so they never cost a key.
A page dresses a control as a link, or the other way roundA control gives a page no power: its click is the MOUSE event every click sends. A link's destination is shown only outside the page. Controls are bounded and checked like every triplet.
A forged cursor or a forged receiver's controlA receiver draws the cursor in a style no page can draw, only in rows 1 to 23, and its own controls are outside the page.
A compromised part of a receiver typesThe part that draws sends intents, and the trusted part alone encodes, for the channel it already lets keys go to. The part that reads the network holds only that channel's keys and bounded messages. A keyboard process is a hostile person at the keys: bounded keys, no modifiers, and the rate.
Someone photographs a phone keyboard's QR codeIt shows only when asked for, with its warning. A PIN is offered, and there is one phone.
A session is taken over after a reconnectionA reconnection is a new session with nothing carried over. With RESUME, only a connection showing the session's token, 128 bits new in each WELCOME, resumes it, and only while no connection holds it. Identity, if any, is the channel's cookies.
A resumed session acts on a key twice, or loses oneEvery message of input units is numbered by the count both sides keep, and the channel drops what it has seen.
A hostile programEvery line it sends is checked whole, its queues are bounded, and one program serves every viewer, so there is no process per viewer to exhaust.
A channel learns what is readAccepted. A selection tells a channel which page and sub-page each viewer reads, as a request tells a web server. A compromised interface could signal what it saw on one channel through what it selects on another.
A stolen session cookieAccepted for now. It works for whoever holds it. Device-bound sessions are the later answer.

Privacy. A receiver MUST NOT log keys, and nothing of them persists past the window. A channel learns where the viewer's pointer and fingers are over its own open page while keys go to it, and nothing elsewhere. The server's figures identify no viewer. What a phone keyboard's relay learns is in the phone keyboard.

The remaining risks, most serious first: plain ws://, likely where it is used; a compromised interface, unlikely, with a medium consequence; an exploited keyboard process, the same; a QR code photographed on a public screen, medium on both counts; and an imitation of the cursor or of a receiver's controls, medium in likelihood and low in consequence.

5 The downstream record set

DOC-1782 · section

5.1 Downstream is a T42 stream

DOC-1948 · concept · informative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-494, REQ-1480, REQ-1501

Everything a channel sends a receiver is teletext. The binary messages, joined in the order sent, are a valid plain T42 file, with nothing between packets and nothing added to them, and no text message is ever sent. A recording of a connection plays in any decoder.

Each message is a whole number of 42-byte packets, of these kinds.

FrameHoldsDelivery
Page frameExactly one sub-page, whole or as changed rowsOnly to the connection whose selection that sub-page is
RecordA record on data channel 12: everything that is not a page, sound among itIn order
Raw packetsPackets a program sent as they are: a recording, or a generated stationIn order, never coalesced

What most viewers read is not the broadcast: page frames give a receiver the pages it selects, and a server never claims otherwise. Raw packets are the opposite: they keep the order a program gave them, nothing in them is coalesced, and a recording played back this way keeps a broadcast's own behaviour, its rotation included.

A page update replaces what it covers, and a row packet belongs to the last header sent. So the unit that can be delayed, dropped or resent is the page frame, never a single packet, and a frame carries its own header so that it stands alone. Over a WebSocket, frames need no sequence numbers: TCP is ordered, and the server knows what each connection was sent. A transport that lost or reordered data would carry its sequence number as a record on data channel 12, never outside the packets, which have no spare field for one.

5.2 Page frames

DOC-1949 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-1032, REQ-1065, REQ-1501, REQ-1504, REQ-1553

A page frame is exactly one sub-page. No event sends two sub-pages in one frame, of the same page or of different pages. A page with one sub-page is a page whose list has one entry, sent the same way. A frame is at most one sub-page, 3,108 bytes before its LINKS record.

A sub-page is its header, rows 1 to 23, row 24 and the packets X/26 to X/28. The header is in serial magazine mode, C11 set, so the stream is valid teletext.

A whole frame has the erase bit, C4, set, so that a sub-page selected again keeps nothing of an older version a receiver's store may hold. It is in the order of Annex B.2: any M/29 packets, the header, X/27, X/28, X/26, then the rows, a row of spaces not being sent. The first sub-page on a connection, the first after its selection changes, and every sub-page whose page or sub-code differs from the one last sent go whole.

A frame of changed rows is sent only for a later version of the very sub-page, same page number and same sub-code, the connection was last sent. It is the header with C4 clear, then the whole group of packets numbered above 24 when any of them changed, then the rows that changed. A receiver keeps every row the frame does not carry. Should a row be gone from the new version, the sub-page goes whole. Nothing is sent when nothing differs.

The header's text is the sub-page's, sent like any row. The server never rewrites it. Columns 0 to 7 of row 0 are the receiver's.

Links and the cursor follow. In the same binary message, a frame is followed by that sub-page's LINKS record when it has Link Extension links, and then, once the session is welcomed, by a cursor record. A change of links alone is a frame of the header with C4 clear, then its LINKS record.

Keeping the last-sent sub-page. A server keeps, for each connection, the one sub-page it last sent it, in memory only, and takes changes against that. It is dropped when the selection changes and when the connection closes, is never shared between connections, and is never served to anyone.

A selected sub-page that a program sends again goes to the connection whatever its focus: a selected page is active.

Rests on: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 9.6.1 Packets X/27/0 to X/27/3 for Editorial Linking; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 9.3.1.1 Page number

5.3 What a connection is sent, and when

DOC-1950 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-1472, REQ-1474, REQ-1481, REQ-1502

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1481 Every selection has an ending: when a program does not answer in time, or is not running, the viewer is told the channel is unavailable and the connection stays open (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

WhenWhat the connection is sent
At connectionWELCOME, and nothing more until the program answers connected, which the server holds for the first SELECT. Then the channel's index records, the one sub-page the selection shows, its LINKS record when it has links, and its page's SUBPAGES records when the program sends a list.
The selection changes, by SELECT or the program's page redirectThe one sub-page the new selection shows, whole, with its LINKS record, and, if the connection lacks the list's current version, its page's SUBPAGES records
The program sends a new version of the selected sub-pageThat sub-page as changed rows, followed by its LINKS record when it has links
The program sends any other sub-pageNothing: a sub-page reaches a connection only when it is that connection's selection
The program changes the selected page's sub-page listThe new SUBPAGES records
The program ends the selection with deniedDENIED with its status, at once
The program does not answer within 5,000 ms, or is not runningDENIED 503
No SELECT within FIRST_SELECTION_WAIT_MS of connecting, or a receiver with no SELECT capabilityThe sub-page the program's reply gives for the channel's initial page, which is then the connection's selection, and no other
A SELECT repeats a selection DENIED endedAs a new selection, with a new number and a new deadline
A message of input units is read, with a selection madeThe next page frame ends with a cursor record that carries the acknowledgement. With no frame within 250 ms of the first input it covers, a cursor record goes alone.

The program is the only source of content: the server answers nothing from a store, and keeps no page, sub-page, index or version between connections or across a restart.

A connection's sub-page never changes by itself. A program that sends another sub-page of the selected page does not change what is shown: it is not forwarded.

Not built. How a selected sub-page's object pages reach a receiver now that a program is the only source is not designed.

Rests on: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 9.6.1 Packets X/27/0 to X/27/3 for Editorial Linking; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause A.1 Page numbering

5.4 The records

DOC-1951 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-36, REQ-1462, REQ-1480

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-36 Records (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-1462 Only packets of the allowed kinds downstream; a receiver discards, counts and reports the rest (tested, not recorded as met on main).

Everything a channel says beyond its pages is a record of the record frame, carried on data channel 12 in packets 4/31, which a decoder that knows none of them skips. A record has a type of its own in the frame, so a receiver that reads only some types skips the others by the type byte alone, before reading any field.

TypeRecordFragmentsHolds
1, 2, 3OBJECT, DATA, CONTROLAnySound
4CursorOneWhere the cursor is, and what has been acknowledged
5ControlOneWELCOME
6IndexOne or moreThe channel's magazines and pages
7LOGOne or moreA log line for the channel's author
8SET_COOKIEOne or moreA cookie set while the connection is open
9LINKSOne or moreA sub-page's Link Extension links
10SUBPAGESOne or moreA page's list of sub-pages
11REDIRECTOne or moreMoves a connection's viewer
12DENIEDOneA selection refused

The NeoTeletext server sends its records on audio service 0, and the record id starts at 0 with WELCOME and counts on from one record to the next on a connection, whatever its type, modulo 256. A value may be skipped, so a receiver uses the id only to gather a record's fragments. An encoder checks what it writes with its reader, so that a server cannot send what a receiver refuses.

Each record's layout is defined once, in its own item in this part, and the reasons each has a type of its own are that the control record is one fragment, 26 bytes of body, and the others need more.

5.5 Records of several fragments, and records a receiver cannot use

DOC-1952 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-40, REQ-494, REQ-1462

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-40 Broadcast carriage (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-1462 Only packets of the allowed kinds downstream; a receiver discards, counts and reports the rest (tested, not recorded as met on main).

Fragments. A record of more than 34 bytes is cut into fragments of 34 bytes, the last padded with zeros, at most 241 fragments, a record of at most 8,192 bytes. Each fragment is one packet with the record id, its index and the CRC-16. A channel sends a record's fragments in order, fragment 0 first, in one binary message or in consecutive ones. A receiver reassembles them by record id, and knows the record's length, and so its fragments, from fragment 0's frame header.

A record a receiver cannot use, a frame whose CRC-32, version or length fails, a field outside its range, text that breaks its rule, or bytes after the last field, is discarded whole and nothing of it applied. Each of its packets is counted as an invalid packet, and the connection is not closed. A LINKS record refused leaves its sub-page shown with no Link Extension links.

A record of a type a receiver does not read is skipped, and not counted.

5.6 The control record: WELCOME

DOC-1953 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-205, REQ-210

Type 5. The body is one control message, encoded as a receiver's events are, 0xFF, a type, its fields, in at most 26 bytes, so that the record with its 8 bytes of frame is the 34 bytes of one fragment.

TypeNameFields
0x01WELCOMEversion uv; caps uv, a subset of the receiver's; and with RESUME in caps, session u8 (0 new, 1 resumed, others a protocol error), token of 16 bytes, and when resumed taken u16 big-endian, the input count the channel had taken
0x02 to 0x04NoneWithdrawn, once input modes. Reserved, and a record of one is ignored.
0x40 to 0x7FExtensionsA length, then a payload, skipped when unknown

Bytes left over, a body over 26 bytes, or bit 16 set in caps refuse the record. A capability above bit 27 would need a WELCOME of its own design.

5.7 The cursor record

DOC-1954 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-195, REQ-196, REQ-209, REQ-212

Type 4, always one fragment: 16 bytes, the frame and a body of 8 bytes.

Body byteFieldRange
0 and 1page, big-endian0x100 to 0x8FF
2 and 3acknowledged, big-endianThe input count acted on, modulo 65,536, or 0 for none yet
4row1 to 23, or 255 for no cursor. Row 0 is the header, and row 24 the Fastext row.
5column0 to 39
6reservedWritten 0, ignored when read
7flags4 BLOCK, a block rather than a bar. 1 and 2 are reserved, written 0 and ignored when read. Any other is refused.

No cursor requires column and flags 0. A record that breaks any rule is refused whole. A cursor is optional: a game sends none, or one with no cursor only to acknowledge. A receiver takes a cursor record wherever it comes, alone or in a message with the packets of an answer, and shows it only on the page it names, in a style no page can draw, and only while keys go to its channel.

A cursor record MUST NOT move a viewer, whatever page it names: one still in flight when a viewer selects another page names the page they left.

A page file may carry its cursor as a CU line, CU,row,column,width,flags[,acknowledged], in decimal, for the page that holds it. The reader keeps the last such line, and reports and drops a line it cannot read. width keeps its place and means nothing: it is read as a number and not kept. The engine's page encoder does not turn the line into a packet: a receiver reads it from the page it already has.

5.8 The index record

DOC-1955 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-514, REQ-1477

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-514 `contents.txt` (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

Type 6: the channel's magazines and pages, as a program gives them. A live channel has no contents.txt, and its index comes as these records, at connection and whenever it changes. Integers are big-endian. Text is a u16 byte length then that many bytes of UTF-8, with no control character and at most 120 characters, so at most 480 bytes.

BytesFieldRange
0 and 1generationAny: the same in every record of one sending, and another in the next, the sender counting on by one and wrapping
2 and 3part0 to parts - 1
4 and 5parts1 to 2,030: a record for each of the 2,022 pages and one for each magazine's name alone
6magazine1 to 8
7 onnameText, 0 to 120 characters: empty when it has none
ThenPagesZero or more, to the end of the body, in ascending page number
Field of a pageRange
page, u160x100 to 0x8FF, of the record's magazine, never a reserved number: mFD, mFE, mFF, 1BE or 1F0
subpages, u161 to 8,190: how many sub-pages the page has
title, text1 to 120 characters
section, text0 to 120 characters: the heading the page is listed under, empty for none

Splitting. A record holds one magazine's pages, as many as fit in 1,016 bytes of body, and a magazine with more goes on in the next record. The records of a sending are numbered in the order of their magazines and pages, so no page is in two records of one sending. A magazine's name is given in its first record of a sending, and the records after it give an empty name, which keeps the name given. A record may hold no pages, and then carries only the magazine's name.

Reassembling. A receiver keeps the index it has and the sending it is receiving.

  1. A record of the generation it is receiving, whose parts agrees with that sending's first record, adds its pages to the sending. A record of another generation begins a new sending, and the one in progress is abandoned.
  2. Each page a record lists is shown at once, added to the index or replacing what was there.
  3. When every part has arrived, the sending is the whole index, and every page and magazine it did not list is removed.
  4. A partial sending removes nothing.

For a viewer, a section begins wherever a page's section differs from the page before it.

5.9 The LOG record

DOC-1956 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-190

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-190 A channel's log lines (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

Type 7: a log line for the channel's author, as a TTI LG line holds one, shown only in a developer's view, never on a page.

BytesFieldRange
0levelE an error, W a warning, or I information
1 and 2page, big-endianThe page the line concerns, 0x100 to 0x8FF, or 0 for none
3 to the endtextUTF-8, 1 to 200 bytes, with no control character and no bidirectional formatting character

A receiver passes on at most 16 LOG records for each page frame it receives, and 16 before the first, and drops the rest, so that a channel cannot flood a console.

5.10 The SET_COOKIE record

DOC-1957 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-495

Type 8: a cookie set while the connection is open. The body is one Set-Cookie header field's value, exactly as an HTTP response carries it after Set-Cookie: (RFC 6265bis section 4.1), of 1 to 1,016 bytes of printable ASCII, 0x20 to 0x7E.

A receiver applies the line to its cookie jar with the same parser and the same rules as a Set-Cookie header of the push connection's own upgrade response, for the push connection's address: host-only, and scoped to the channel's folder. A line the jar refuses is reported as a refused cookie.

A sender MUST NOT write the line by hand: the server builds it from a program's typed cookie message with the engine's encoder, which refuses a name or value the parser would refuse.

5.11 The LINKS record

DOC-1958 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-1494

Type 9: a sub-page's Link Extension links, which packets cannot carry: key links to other services, to addresses and to the web, page-number links and text links. It holds every Link Extension link of the sub-page, the key links to its own service too, so that the links a receiver holds are exactly the page's. The page's Fastext links and row 24 still travel in its packets X/27/0 and X/24.

Integers are big-endian. A target is a u16 byte length, then 1 to 8,004 bytes of printable ASCII with no space and no comma, in any form the Link Extension allows.

BytesFieldRange
0 and 1page0x100 to 0x8FF: the sub-page's page
2 and 3sub0x0000 to 0x3F7E: its sub-code
4part0 to parts - 1
5parts1 to 21, the most links a sub-page has, 5 + 8 + 8
6keys0 to 5, then that many key links
Thennumbers0 to 8, then that many page-number links
Thentexts0 to 8, then that many text links, and nothing after
LinkFields
Key link (LN)key u8: R, G, Y, C or I; target; label: a u8 length, then 0 to 9 bytes of printable ASCII, at most 8 for a target in another service or at an address, and none for I
Page-number link (LK)number u16, a page number 0x100 to 0x8FF the page shows; target, never a web: one
Text link (LT)row u8, 1 to 23; column u8, 0 to 39; length u8, at least 1, with column + length at most 40; target

Bounds across the parts. Over all the parts of one sending, a sub-page has at most five key links, each key once and at most four of them coloured, at most eight page-number links, each number once, and at most eight text links. A record that would break one is refused.

Splitting. The links go in order, key links first, then page-number links, then text links, as many in each record as fit in 8,184 bytes of body. A link is never split.

What a receiver does. The server sends a sub-page's LINKS records straight after its frame, every time it sends the sub-page, whenever it has any Link Extension link. Part 0 replaces every link the receiver held for that page and sub-code, and later parts add to it. A sub-page sent without a LINKS record has no Link Extension links, and a receiver drops those it held for it. A receiver reads the links exactly as it reads a TTI file's LN, LK and LT lines.

5.12 The SUBPAGES record

DOC-1959 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-1503

Type 10: a page's list of sub-pages. Sub-pages are never sent with their page, so a receiver learns a page's list from this record, for the list, the count, the place shown, such as "3/12", and stepping. It is never the sub-pages themselves. It is sent when the connection selects the page or one of its sub-pages and does not hold the list's current version, and again whenever the list changes while the selection is the page or one of its sub-pages.

Integers are big-endian.

BytesFieldRange
0 and 1page0x100 to 0x8FF
2 and 3versionAny: the same in every record of one sending, and changed whenever the list changes
4 and 5part0 to parts - 1
6 and 7parts1 to 8,190
8 and 9count1 to 8,190: the page's sub-pages, the same in every record of the sending
ThenEntriesOne or more, to the end of the body, in the page's order

Each entry is sub u16, a sub-code 0x0000 to 0x3F7E, each at most once in the list, then title: a u8 byte length and 0 to 160 bytes of UTF-8, at most 40 characters, with no control character, empty for none.

Splitting. The entries go in the page's order, ascending sub-code with 0000 first when present, as many in each record as fit in 1,016 bytes of body, at least six, and an entry is never split.

Reassembling, as the index: a record of another version of the page begins a new sending. A sending is complete when every part has arrived and its entries number count, and then it is the page's whole list. A partial sending shows what it has and removes nothing.

Rests on: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 3.1 Definitions; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause A.1 Page numbering

5.13 The REDIRECT record

DOC-1960 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-212, REQ-1688

Type 11: what a program sends to move a connection's viewer to another page of the channel, or to another channel. It is the only thing that moves a viewer. An HTTP redirect from a web server is another thing, and is not carried here.

The body is one address, as an HTTP Location is, of printable ASCII with no space and no fragment. It is either a place of this channel, 302 or 302/0002, with a query when the selection it moves keeps one, at most 8,009 bytes; or a whole address, an http or https address the strict address parser accepts, at most 8,000 bytes. A place on a reserved page is refused.

  • A place. The server sends the record and records the page as the connection's selection, with its query, exactly as it would a SELECT, and the program is then sent selected for it. A receiver opens that page and sub-page, whether or not its page has the focus, and sends SELECT for it, with the query. A redirect to a sub-page names the selection as it stands, so that SELECT repeats it and asks for nothing more. A redirect to a page, sub-code 3F7F, is answered with a sub-page, which then is the selection, so the receiver's SELECT of the page is a new one: the program hears selected again, and the receiver is sent that sub-page again, whole.
  • A whole address. The server sends the record and changes nothing else: the selection stands until the receiver leaves. A receiver that opens channels puts the address through its own address rules, which alone decide: a redirect leads only to an http or https address, never a local file, a web: hand-off or the receiver's own pages. It shows a redirect notice, and then opens the address as another channel. A receiver that cannot open channels says that the channel redirected and to where, and stays.

The viewer's own choice overrides a redirect, and a receiver bounds how many redirects it follows in a row with no input from the viewer.

5.14 The DENIED record

DOC-1961 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-1481

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1481 Every selection has an ending: when a program does not answer in time, or is not running, the viewer is told the channel is unavailable and the connection stays open (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

Type 12: a selection refused by the channel's program, at once, or a program that did not answer by the deadline. It gives every selection a defined answer, as a status gives a web request one. Integers are big-endian.

BytesFieldRange
0 and 1statusA real HTTP status number: 403 not allowed, 404 no such page, 429 a limit reached, or 503 the channel's program is not answering
2 to the endplaceThe place refused, as SELECT carried it with no query, or as the selection then stood, so a page already answered is named by its sub-page, 100/0000, at most 8 bytes of printable ASCII, or nothing when the whole channel is unavailable

The connection stays open. A receiver says so to the viewer in words of its own, and takes the next selection as usual. A connection with no selection, whose program did not answer, is sent DENIED 503 with no place.

5.15 What arrives as one unit

DOC-1962 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-200, REQ-1494, REQ-1503

A receiver applies these whole, or not at all.

  • A page frame and its links: the frame's packets, then that sub-page's LINKS records when it has Link Extension links. The next frame, or a frame with no LINKS record, is what says it has none. A frame is applied before its LINKS records arrive, and a sub-page shows no Link Extension links until they do.
  • A sending of the index, or of a SUBPAGES list: whole when every part has arrived. A partial one shows what it has and removes nothing.
  • A LINKS sending: part 0 replaces what was held, and later parts add to it.
  • A record of several fragments: whole by its CRC, or discarded.
  • A WebSocket message of the input stream: whole items, read alone.

Nothing else is a unit. A selection's sub-page and its SUBPAGES record may arrive in either order, and a receiver holds neither back for the other.

5.16 The wire vectors
