Checking it. The triplet found MUST be an Object Definition of the same type, whose usage bits include the level: 01 for 2.5, 10 for 3.5, and 11 for both. The object number bits are not compared. The body runs to the next Object Definition or Termination Marker, at most 507 triplets, crossing from triplet 12 to the next packet and from packet 25 to X/26/0, and never onto another sub-page. A packet missing from the body makes that object absent, and leaves others.

What may invoke what.

InvokerMay invoke
Local enhancement dataActive, Adaptive and Passive, from local, POP or GPOP
Active objectAdaptive and Passive
Adaptive objectPassive
Passive objectNothing
Default objectsPOP and GPOP objects of any type, never local

A local object is invoked only by the page's own data or another local object. An object invoked from inside a public object is found through the displayed page's links.

The three types. An Active object acts as local enhancement data, its attributes running to the end of the row on every character. An Adaptive object starts with the underlying attributes, and on each row its changes affect its own and the Level 1 characters between the first and the rightmost cell any of its triplets addresses on that row, found by scanning it first; to the right, the underlying attributes apply again. A Passive object starts each invocation from white on black, normal size, steady, unboxed, not concealed, contiguous; its attributes persist across rows and apply only to cells it writes.

Priority. Where several sources write a cell, Passive objects win, then Adaptive, then local data, then Active, then the Level 1 page. Between two of one type, the latest invoked wins, default objects included.

Restrictions. Full Screen Colour, Full Row Colour and Address Display Row 0 are ignored in Adaptive and Passive objects. An element placed outside rows 0 to 24 or columns 0 to 39, or in row 0 columns 0 to 7, is discarded, and the object continues.

Limits. At most 127 invocations of each type a page, 15 objects open at once, and 15 opened in succession with nothing displayable between. Further invocations are ignored and counted. Enhancement processing allocates nothing.

Defined by: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 13 Objects; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 13.1 Object definitions; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 13.2 Display priority order; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 13.3 Active Objects; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 13.4 Adaptive Objects; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 13.5 Passive Objects; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 13.6 Object invocation; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 13.7 Screen position of Object elements; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 10.5.1.2 Pointer Table; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 28 Function of Row Address triplets; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause A.7.1 Overlapping rules; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause A.8 Number of objects per page; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause C.2 Rules for determining priorities of spacing and non-spacing attributes; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 10.5.1.1 Page Format; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 12.5.3 Data transmission and addressing; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause A.4 Addressing order for enhancement triplets

7.5.11 Which pages a page's objects come from, and default objects

DOC-1842 · reference · normative · requirements REQ-344, REQ-345, REQ-346, REQ-347

Links. A page's GPOP and POP come from its own X/27/4, and at Level 3.5 also X/27/5, when present, whatever the MOT's flag says, and from the MOT otherwise. X/27/4 links 0 to 3 are by position the GPOP, POP, GDRCS and DRCS links; a link whose function bits do not match is ignored, and the last two are not used. At Level 3.5, X/27/4 links 4 and 5 and X/27/5 links 0 and 1 take their role from their function bits. A link is used only when bit 11 of its first triplet is 1, bit 1 of its second is 0, and its page validity includes the level. Validity 00, sixteen flags at 0, or page FF mean no link. A duplicate is ignored. Sub-page flags are only hints.

The MOT. A decoder uses page mFE, sub-page S1 = 0, of the page's magazine. From the page's byte pair it takes the global object flag, only a hint, and the POP link number. Links come from X/19 and X/20 at Level 2.5, and X/22 to X/24 at Level 3.5. When X/22 or X/24 is missing, the Level 2.5 links are used and the page is processed at Level 2.5. When only X/23 is missing, POP links 4 to 7 come from X/20. The magazine of a link is absolute, 0 meaning 8, and page FF means not used. A link's count n means sub-pages 0 to n are assumed, and one beyond it is used if stored.

Default objects. A page with no X/26 that carries a display triplet takes the default objects of the MOT: up to two from its magazine's GPOP link, whatever the page's flag says, and up to two from its POP link, in the order GPOP 1, GPOP 2, POP 1, POP 2, each with its origin at row 0, column 0, resolved with N8 = 0. A page whose X/26 holds only Programme Delivery Control triplets keeps them.

Defined by: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 9.6.2 Packets X/27/4 and X/27/5 - Format 1 - for compositional linking in presentation enhancement applications; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 10.5.1.4 Page Number Identification; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 10.6 Magazine Organization Table; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 10.6.1 Page Format; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 10.6.4 Object Links; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 10.6.4.1 Default Object Invocation; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 13.8 Default Objects; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 15 Coding of Packets X/27/4 and X/27/5, Format 1; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 16 Fixed Link Functions of Packets X/27/4 and X/27/5; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 21 Coding of object links within MOT; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause D.1.4 No packet X/27/4; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause G.5 Order of invocation of default objects

7.6 What NeoTeletext does not implement

DOC-1843 · reference · normative · requirements REQ-256, REQ-326, REQ-348, REQ-358, REQ-362, REQ-1643, REQ-1644

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-326 TOP's page numbers (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-348 Enhancement pages are never shown as text (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

These parts of EN 300 706 are not implemented at any level. A page that uses them shows as a receiver without them shows it.

  • DRCS. Nothing of clause 14 is decoded: NeoTeletext wants no form of bitmap graphics. A DRCS Character Invocation (column mode 01101) places nothing, so the Level 1 character beneath shows, and it moves the active position as every column triplet does. A DRCS Mode triplet (row mode 11000) is a reserved row triplet. X/28/1 and M/29/1 are ignored, and of X/28/3 only the page function and coding are read. The DRCS links are not used. A DRCS page is kept as an enhancement page and never shown as text.
  • Font styles. The Font Style triplet (column mode 01110) only sets the column. Bold, italic and proportional spacing are never drawn. Underline stays.
  • Side panels. A page is always 40 columns by 25 rows. Side-panel fields are read past and have no effect, and an object placed past column 39 places nothing. The grid is never wider.
  • TOP navigation. TOP pages are stored as data pages and not used to navigate.
  • Data broadcasting. Pages of EN 300 708 clause 4 are recognised and not decoded.

Defined by: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 14 Dynamically Re-definable Characters; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 12.5 Side Panels; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 10.5.2 DRCS Downloading Pages; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 29 Function of Column Address triplets; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 28 Function of Row Address triplets; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 11.2 Navigation via TOP; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause A.4 Addressing order for enhancement triplets; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause A.8 Number of objects per page

7.7 Extensions to the triplets

DOC-1844 · reference · normative · requirements REQ-216, REQ-283

NeoTeletext adds to the packets of the standard in two places, using values the standard reserves, so that any other decoder treats them as it treats anything reserved.

Column mode 00101, a control. Table 29 reserves this mode. In a page's local enhancement data it declares a run of cells the page answers itself: its address is the first column, D5 to D0 the width, 1 to 40, and D6 joins it to the control on the row above at the same column and width. Its row is the active row, from the Set Active Position or Full Row Colour triplet before it. The mode draws nothing, and a page with controls looks the same everywhere. Its controls travel, are stored and are recorded with its rows, in a page frame, a .t42 file or a TTI page's OL,26 lines.

  • One triplet is one control on its row, so four buttons on a row are four triplets, separate even where they touch. A taller control repeats its triplet on each row below with D6 set.
  • Only the page's local enhancement data declares controls, up to its first Termination Marker or Object Definition. An object declares none.
  • A triplet is dropped and counted, and the others kept, for a row outside 1 to 23, no width, a run past column 39, a cell already in a control, a joined run under no control of its column and width, or more than 32 controls on the page.

Designations in Tables 32 to 34. NeoTeletext's own character sets use values the tables reserve.

The behaviour of controls under a pointer belongs to the upstream protocol.

Defined by: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 29 Function of Column Address triplets; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 12.3.4 Column Address triplets

7.8 Character sets

DOC-1845 · section

7.8.1 How characters are held

DOC-1846 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-70, REQ-79, REQ-423

Verified by inspection. REQ-70 Internal character representation: Read at engine 6112177: `CharRef` in `crates/neoteletext-core/src/charset.rs` holds a `CharSet` (G0, G2 or G3), a 7-bit code and the diacritical mark's number, and `Glyph` in `level1/cell.rs` holds G1 as its 6-bit pattern, so a cell holds no Unicode value; `to_unicode` is called at output and to choose a joined form. No test can check this, because the requirement is about how the decoder holds characters and any representation could pass a test of what it draws.

Inside a decoder each character is a character set identifier and a seven-bit code, never a Unicode value, with a diacritical mark's number when it has one. Conversion to Unicode happens only at output, because some teletext characters have no exact Unicode equivalent.

Any internal character converts to a Unicode scalar value or a short sequence, or to a documented fallback. Text and terminal output MUST pass an allow-list of printable characters: C0 and C1 controls, DEL and every bidirectional control are removed, and a terminal renderer emits only its own escape sequences.

7.8.2 Selecting a page's character sets

DOC-1847 · reference · normative · requirements REQ-72, REQ-78, REQ-264

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-72 Selecting the character set of a page (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

The national option is always the header's C12 to C14 at Levels 1 and 1.5. The group, bits 11 to 14 of the Table 32 value, comes from the page's X/28/0 Format 1 when present and correct, then the latest M/29/0 of its magazine, then the receiver's configured region, by default 0000.

At Levels 2.5 and 3.5 the national option comes from bits 8 to 10 of the highest-priority designation when one is present, and C12 to C14 otherwise, in the order of Annex C.4 rule 1: X/28/0 Format 1, X/28/4 at Level 3.5, M/29/0, M/29/4 at Level 3.5.

The G0 and G2 sets in force come from Table 32. When the entry is reserved, the same three national option bits are tried in group 0000, and when that is also reserved, English is used. The fallback is counted as a diagnostic.

The designation also sets the default sets used by X/26 characters, and the second G0 set used by ESC through Table 33. With no second set, or designation 1111111, ESC only occupies its cell. The national option bits do not apply to the second set. X/28/1 and M/29/1 never affect character sets.

A Modified G0 and G2 Character Set Designation triplet (mode 01000) selects a pair from Table 34, and reserved values are ignored. It lasts to the end of the row in local data and Active objects, and to the last character cell of the object in Adaptive and Passive objects, or to the next such triplet. It never affects the Level 1 page. At Level 2.5 a designation that would make more than two pairs on a page, counting the default, is ignored. At Level 3.5 there is no limit.

Defined by: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 15.1 Overview of designation requirements; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 15.2 Designation of default G0 and G2 sets and national option sub-sets; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 15.3 Second G0 Set Designation and National Option Set Selection; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 15.4 Modified G0 and G2 Character Set Designation; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 32 Function of Default G0 and G2 Character Set Designation and National Option; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 33 Function of Second Language Designation and National Option Selection bits in packets; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 34 Function of the Modified G0 and G2 Character Set Designation triplet; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause C.4 Interpretation of character set and DRCS table changes at enhanced levels; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause E.9 Selection of national option sub-sets; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause E.5 Character set designation

7.8.3 The sets of the standard

DOC-1848 · reference · normative · requirements REQ-71, REQ-73, REQ-74, REQ-75, REQ-76, REQ-77, REQ-80

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-77 Diacritical marks (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

Every set of clause 15 is implemented as a code table with its Unicode mapping: Latin G0 with all 13 national option subsets of Table 36, the three Cyrillic G0 options, Greek, Arabic and Hebrew G0, the Latin, Cyrillic, Greek and Arabic G2 sets, G1 and G3.

The base forms of Table 35 are used whenever X/26 refers to a Latin G0 character. Four national option glyphs are too small in the standard to identify, and are mapped as below. The Turkish currency sign at 2/3 is mapped to U+20BA.

PositionCharacterUnicode
Polish 5/BZ with strokeU+01B5
Polish 7/Dl with strokeU+0142
English 6/0Long dashU+2014
English 7/CDouble vertical barU+2016

Positions the standard leaves undefined show a space: Latin G2 5/9, 5/A, 5/B and 6/5, and G3 6/E, 6/F, 7/E and 7/F. G3 position 5/F, which shows the background colour, converts to a space.

Mosaics as text. Take n = (code AND 0x1F) OR ((code AND 0x40) shifted right by 1). Contiguous mosaics map as: 0 to a space, 21 to U+258C, 42 to U+2590, 63 to U+2588, and the rest to U+1FB00 to U+1FB3B in increasing n, skipping 21 and 42. Separated mosaics map to U+1CE50 + n.

G3 as text follows Unicode's TELTXTG3.TXT mapping.

Diacritical marks. Column 4 of the G2 set gives the sixteen marks. The standard does not name 4/9 and 4/C, and NeoTeletext treats them as U+0323 and U+0332. In pixels a precomposed glyph is used when the font has it, and otherwise the mark overlays the base. In text, the base and combining mark are normalised to Form C. Composition applies only to Latin G0 bases, or as the extended sets say. On other sets the base shows without the mark, and a diagnostic counts it.

Defined by: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 15.6 Alphanumeric Character Sets; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 15.7 Graphics Character Sets; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 35 Latin G0 Primary Set; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 36 Latin National Option Sub-sets; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 37 Latin G2 Supplementary Set; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 38 Cyrillic G0 Primary Set - Option 1 - Serbian/Croatian; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 41 Cyrillic G2 Supplementary Set; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 44 Arabic G0 Primary Set; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 46 Hebrew G0 Primary Set; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 47 G1 Block Mosaics Set; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 48 G3 Smooth Mosaics and Line Drawing Set; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause E.10 Characters including diacritical marks; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause E.3 Use of packets X/26 in Level 1.5 transmissions

7.8.4 NeoTeletext's own character sets

DOC-1849 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-81, REQ-82, REQ-83, REQ-84, REQ-85

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-82 The code tables of neoteletext's own sets (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-84 Joining Arabic-script letters (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-85 Thai and Lao (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

The feature extended-sets, in the default build and the browser's, adds sets for about 55 languages the standard does not serve. Each is one byte per cell, read as EN 300 706 defines, and is designated by a value Tables 32 to 34 reserve. The allocation is NeoTeletext's own, so another decoder takes the fallback of Table 32's reserved values, as does a build without the feature. Every new set has Latin English as its second G0 set, switched by ESC.

ValuesDefault G0 setG2 set
0101 000 to 0101 101Latin with an option: Hausa and Fula; West African; Ewe; Kabyle and Tamazight; Azerbaijani; Kanuri and MossiLatin
0101 110Yoruba and Igbo, a whole setLatin
0101 111Vietnamese, a whole setVietnamese
0111 000 to 0111 011Cyrillic: Ukrainian, Belarusian, Macedonian, SerbianCyrillic
0111 101 to 0111 111Cyrillic: Kyrgyz and Mongolian, Tatar, TajikCyrillic
1001 000 to 1001 111Arabic script: Persian and Dari, Urdu, Pashto, Sindhi, Sorani Kurdish, Uyghur, Balochi, Arabic in plain lettersArabic
1011 000GeorgianLatin
1011 001ArmenianArmenian
1011 010TifinaghLatin
1100 000Latin English, with Thai secondThai
1100 010Thai, with English secondThai
1100 100LaoLao

A Level 1 page gets its group from the receiver's region, as for every group. Every G0 set has the space at 2/0 and the block at 7/F.

Marks. A diacritical-mark triplet takes its mark from column 4 of the page's current G2 set when that column holds marks (Latin, Armenian and Vietnamese), and from Latin G2 otherwise. The Vietnamese G2 4/C is U+0309.

Arabic-script sets. Each holds a letter once, and the receiver chooses its form. After a page's grid is composed, each row is passed once from right to left. A letter's previous letter is the cell to its right, and its next the cell to its left, when each holds a letter of the same set. It joins its previous letter when that is dual-joining or join-causing, and its next when it is itself dual-joining and the next is dual-joining, right-joining or join-causing. Joining neither is isolated, both medial, only the previous final, and only the next initial. Joining types are Unicode's ArabicShaping.txt, with lam-alef right-joining. Rows stay in visual order. Digits keep 3/0 to 3/9.

Thai and Lao. The Thai sets are those of the SAA5246AP/S, and Lao's codes come from the 1990 World System Teletext specification. A mark is an ordinary character in its own cell, and the receiver neither overlays nor reorders. A G2 combination converts to Unicode as a combining sequence with the vowel sign first.

The code tables of these sets follow.

Rests on: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 29 Function of Column Address triplets; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 32 Function of Default G0 and G2 Character Set Designation and National Option; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 33 Function of Second Language Designation and National Option Selection bits in packets; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 34 Function of the Modified G0 and G2 Character Set Designation triplet

7.8.5 The code tables of NeoTeletext's own sets

DOC-2028 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-81, REQ-82, REQ-83, REQ-84, REQ-85

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-82 The code tables of neoteletext's own sets (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-84 Joining Arabic-script letters (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-85 Thai and Lao (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

These are the code tables of NeoTeletext's own character sets, which no standard holds. A decoder holds them as it holds the tables of EN 300 706. They are written from the engine's own tables, so they cannot differ from what a receiver on the engine does.

Each table gives its set's characters in Unicode. The bytes shown first are those characters in UTF-8, code 2/0 first. Each row after them is a column of the table, named as the standard names a position, column then row, and lists its sixteen cells in order. A cell is the code point in hexadecimal, then the character, so 01B0 ư in the fourth place of column 2 says that code 2/3 of the set is U+01B0. A cell the set leaves empty is a space. A cell of two code points is two marks drawn as one, as a Thai or Lao G2 combination is.

Latin with an option. Each is Table 35 with the 13 national option positions replaced, as an option of Table 36 replaces them. Only those positions are listed.

Whole Latin sets, and Cyrillic. A whole set holds its letters itself, so that an X/26 diacritical mark finds them. The Vietnamese G2 set goes with the Vietnamese G0 set. The Cyrillic sets are Tables 38, 39 and 40 with some positions changed, so that letters keep their codes, and use the standard's Cyrillic G2 set.

Arabic script. Each set holds a letter once, and a receiver chooses the form to draw by the joining rule. All of them use the standard's Arabic G2 set.

Other alphabets, Thai and Lao. Georgian and Tifinagh use Latin G2. Armenian, Thai and Lao have G2 sets of their own, and those of Thai and Lao hold the marks and the combinations of marks.

Rests on: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 15.6 Alphanumeric Character Sets

7.9 Fonts

DOC-1850 · section

7.9.1 The one compiled font

DOC-1851 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-73, REQ-121, REQ-132, REQ-383, REQ-401

A receiver built on the engine carries one font, Noto Teletext, a conversion of Google's Noto fonts to the 12 by 20 cell. It is compiled in, no feature chooses it, and no other font is compiled in. Each glyph is 12 pixels by 20 lines, the whole cell: capitals stand on lines 2 to 15, ten pixels wide, with the baseline on line 16. It covers every character of the standard's 24 sets, every spacing mark and composed Latin letter, and with extended-sets the sets of the extended sets with their joined forms and marks.

It is the default font of every page and the only font of the receiver's own drawing: row 0's status area, its row 24 messages and any confirmation. It is drawn line for line, and is never rounded.

A character of a supported set that the font lacks is a defect in the font. Until it is filled, the character is composed from a base and a spacing mark where possible, and otherwise drawn as a visible placeholder: a hollow box on the lines a capital stands on. A renderer MUST NOT draw a wrong character in its place.

Mosaics and G3 characters are not font glyphs. They are drawn by rule.

A binary that holds the font carries the SIL Open Font License 1.1 and each source's copyright line.

7.9.2 Loaded fonts

DOC-1852 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-122, REQ-123, REQ-124, REQ-125, REQ-128, REQ-130, REQ-131

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-123 Where fonts come from (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-124 Font libraries (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-131 A font's conformance for a character set (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

A user may load other fonts. A channel or a page can never name one. A font comes from a local file or from an address the user names, and is parsed only in a confined process, never in the process that draws. A font named with a SHA-256 is refused when its checksum differs.

The form. A font is a BDF file of version 2.1 or 2.2, at most 256 kilobytes, with lines of at most 512 bytes, accepted only when: it has FONTBOUNDINGBOX 12 10 0 0 or 12 20 0 0; its properties, at most 64, include CHARSET_REGISTRY "ISO10646", CHARSET_ENCODING "1", and SPACING "C" or "M"; CHARS is at most 1,024, with exactly that many following; every character has an ENCODING that is a Unicode scalar value, never repeated, DWIDTH 12 0, a matching BBX, and a BITMAP of exactly ten or twenty rows of four hexadecimal digits whose last four bits are zero. FONT, SIZE and SWIDTH are checked for form and not used. Anything else refuses the whole file, with its line number.

Which font draws a character. On rows 1 to 24: the user's font for the character's own set, then the compiled font, then the placeholder. neottx render --font fills a slot that comes first. Row 0's status area always uses the compiled font.

Drawing. A 12 by 10 font is drawn with each line repeated, and a 12 by 20 font line for line. In the 12 by 10 cell a 12 by 20 character shows the upper line of each pair. Attributes apply as to any character. Rounding applies only to a 12 by 10 font whose every row is made of identical pixel pairs, a doubled dot matrix, and only while rounding is on.

Conformance. A font conforms for a character set when the file meets the form, every assigned position of the set has a glyph at the Unicode code point the tables give, the space is blank, and every other assigned character has a pixel set. It is decided from the glyphs alone, and a page never depends on it. neottx font sets FONT reports it.

Libraries. A font library is a folder with an index fonts.txt, at most 64 kilobytes and 1,000 lines. An f line names a font, its title and its SHA-256, and a c line its conformance. A font is fetched only when chosen, and conformance is computed again when it loads.

neottx font convert converts a font of another cell only where that is exact and reversible.

Rests on: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 15.6 Alphanumeric Character Sets

7.10 Drawing

DOC-1853 · section

7.10.1 Cells and rows of pixels

DOC-1854 · reference · normative · requirements REQ-374, REQ-375, REQ-376, REQ-384, REQ-385, REQ-386, REQ-387, REQ-399, REQ-406, REQ-407, REQ-409

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-376 Scaling and aspect (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-399 No scaling in the core; aspect correction at the edge (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

A cell is 12 pixels by 20 lines, and a page 480 by 500 pixels. A second mode draws 12 by 10 cells, a page of 480 by 250, with no rounding: the compiled font has 12 by 20 glyphs, so each cell shows the upper line of each pair of its lines, and a loaded 12 by 10 font is drawn as given. A renderer draws only these two sizes. Scaling belongs to the output, and the 12 by 20 cell matches a 4:3 television when pixels are about 1.23 times, 16/13, wider than tall. A helper scales by whole numbers and may stretch the width by 16/13, and neottx does so with --aspect, off by default.

Double height draws each line of the upper or lower half of the glyph on two output lines. Double width draws the left or right six half-dots of the normal line, each twice, after rounding and mosaic geometry. Double size combines them.

Rounding applies only to a loaded doubled dot-matrix font, in the 12 by 20 mode, by the rule the SAA5050 uses: with A the row drawn and B its neighbour, A | ((A >> 1) & B & ~(B >> 1)) | ((A << 1) & B & ~(B << 1)) on half-dot rows, the upper line of a pair taking the row above and the lower the row below, with rows outside the glyph empty. It is never applied to mosaics, G3 or any 12 by 20 glyph.

Mosaics. In a cell of W half-dots by H lines the two block columns are W/2 wide, and the three block rows 3/10, 4/10 and 3/10 of H: 6, 8 and 6 lines, or 3, 4 and 3. A separated block loses its leftmost dot column and bottom line to background: in the 12 by 20 cell, half-dot columns 0, 1, 6 and 7 and lines 4, 5, 12, 13, 18 and 19.

G3 characters are drawn at every level from the project's own geometry, the sextant geometry of Unicode document L2/19-025 as polygons on halves and thirds of the cell, separately for each cell size. Thirds round down to whole lines. Light strokes are 2 half-dots wide and heavy strokes 4. Position 2/F is a one-dot checkerboard, as the words of Table 48 say, not the pattern of its figure.

Underline is one line of foreground on the last dot line of the cell, across the cell: line 19, or line 9 in the 12 by 10 cell. It is drawn for G0, G1 columns 4 and 5, and G2, never for G3, and for a double-height character only on its bottom half. A render option turns it off for a port that draws its own link underline.

Defined by: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause G.2 Character displays; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 47 G1 Block Mosaics Set; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 48 G3 Smooth Mosaics and Line Drawing Set; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 15.6 Alphanumeric Character Sets

7.10.2 Colours and flash in pixels

DOC-1855 · reference · normative · requirements REQ-377, REQ-380, REQ-381, REQ-395, REQ-398, REQ-402, REQ-403, REQ-404, REQ-408, REQ-413

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-377 Colours (to be rechecked on livedevelopment); REQ-402 The 32-entry Colour Map in output (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

A renderer produces colour indices, never final colours. There are two modes.

The palette mode, for Levels 1 and 1.5: indices 0 to 7 are the eight colours and 8 is transparent. A foreground pixel of a flashing cell with foreground f and background b is 16 + 8 × f + b, and of a concealed cell 80 + 8 × f + b. A palette of 144 colours is then filled for the flash phase and reveal setting, so flash and reveal need no redrawing. A cell both flashing and concealed uses the concealed range while reveal is off.

The resolved mode, for Levels 2.5 and 3.5: Colour Map indices 0 to 31, with flash and reveal applied at render time. For each cell the renderer swaps colours if it is inverted, then applies flash, then applies conceal. The palette mode MUST be refused for a grid that uses a colour outside CLUT 0 or a Level 2.5 flash mode.

Output colours. The Level 1 colours are CLUT 0 of Table 30, each primary 0 or 255 in 8-bit output, and may be replaced by the user. Each twelve-bit entry of a Colour Map converts by multiplying each four-bit value by 17. User colours replace the Table 30 defaults before any page data redefines them. Pictures show as fully transparent pixels where a format allows.

Change. A renderer draws only rows that changed, and a row below a changed double-height row. In the resolved mode a change of flash unit reports exactly the rows whose displayed colours differ, and a change of reveal the rows with concealed cells.

Text output writes each row's 40 cells as UTF-8, with concealed characters as spaces unless revealed, and attributes as spaces. Terminal output takes 24-bit colours from the Colour Map, or the nearest of the eight in 8-colour mode.

Defined by: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 12.4 Colour Map; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 30 Colour Map; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table C.1 Display Priorities

7.10.3 Decided, not built: the glyph line cache

DOC-2124 · reference · informative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-392

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-392 The glyph line cache (not implemented on livedevelopment).

Not built. No code does this. An optional cache is to hold the finished 12-dot mask for every character, every line of the cell and each field, with rounding and mosaic geometry already applied, generated from the cell raster when the decoder starts or at build time as constant data. For the Latin G0 set with its national option characters and the G1 mosaic set, it needs about 8 kilobytes, or about 4 kilobytes when only one field is drawn. With it, drawing one line of one cell is a table lookup and a colour fill. The cell raster stays the reference, and characters not in the cache, such as G2 and G3 overwrites, fall back to it.

7.11 Where the standard leaves a choice

DOC-2122 · reference · informative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-144, REQ-1639, REQ-1641

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-144 Row resolution at Levels 2.5 and 3.5: the per-cell step (not implemented on livedevelopment); REQ-1641 Rendering rulings at Levels 2.5 and 3.5: choosing the colour mode, underline, row 0 transparency, mix mode, still images, G3 glyphs and eight-colour terminals (not implemented on livedevelopment).

The standard leaves these points open. They are the engine's rulings: another decoder may rule otherwise and still read the same pages. Each keeps the work bounded, contains a fault, or takes the reading that never hides displayed text. Objects, Magazine Organization Table links and default objects are behind the feature objects, off by default, and the rulings on them say how they behave when built.

The enhancement processor.

  • A damaged triplet met while an Origin Modifier waits for its invocation discards the offsets.
  • Every permitted invocation counts toward the limit of 127 per object type, whether or not its object is found, so that failed lookups also bound the work.
  • Objects open at once, as Annex A.8 limits them, are counted at the invocation point against the open areas of all earlier objects. An object's area runs from its invocation to the last position any of its moving triplets reaches. The count of successive opens restarts at any character or attribute actually placed.
  • At Level 3.5, a stored Magazine Organization Table lacking packet X/22 or X/24 makes the page fall back to Level 2.5 links, whatever X/27/4 says.
  • X/27/4 and X/27/5 take precedence over the table one role at a time, and a role they do not give comes from the table.
  • Default objects are found through the table's own GPOP and POP pages, and a table link to page FF gives none. A display triplet, which suppresses them, is any character or attribute column triplet, screen or row colour, Address Display Row 0 or invocation met before the first Termination Marker or Object Definition.
  • An object writes only into the 40 columns of the page. Elements outside them are discarded, and the object continues.
  • On a page with neither C5 nor C6 set, both Level 1 box codes are ignored. On a boxed page, one boxed state is shared with the window bit of the Display Attributes triplet, and the later of the two wins.
  • Overlapping Adaptive objects keep one merged attribute record per cell, owned by the latest object to place there.
  • Passive objects apply their attributes in transmission order, as the walk meets them, and a Passive attribute at a discarded position is dropped.
  • Incremental and decremental flash count phases by column from the triplet's column, spaces included.
  • Row 0 is rebuilt only when C7 is clear, always keeping columns 0 to 7. Row 24 is rebuilt only when it is shown and not drawn by the receiver. A page with C10 set, or with an X/28/0 page function other than basic text, keeps its Level 1 rows.
  • A page is taken as an enhancement page, and its rows 1 to 24 are blanked, when its number is xFE or its stored copy carries an X/28 page function of GPOP, POP, GDRCS, DRCS or MOT. A page named only by a link is still shown, so that a wrong link cannot hide a display page.
  • X/26/15 is always processed at Levels 2.5 and 3.5.
  • The lower-row fill cells of double height take the background of the cell above and keep its box membership, with flash and invert cleared.
  • A build counts each kind of fault and the triplets read, and the build recorded last is not counted again, so that the counts measure receptions, not how long a page stayed on screen.

A row, cell by cell. At Levels 2.5 and 3.5 each row is resolved from its Level 1 bytes and the enhancement events on it. For each cell, from column 0 to column 39, in this order:

  1. Apply the set-after attributes recorded at the previous cell.
  2. Apply a set-at Level 1 attribute.
  3. Apply each attribute event of the local enhancement data and of Active objects placed at this cell, in the order of the walk. These win against step 2.
  4. For each Adaptive object whose range on this row includes this cell, start its overlay at its first cell from the underlying record, apply its attribute events here, and use its value for each attribute type it has changed.
  5. Apply the attribute events of each Passive object placed here to that object's own record.
  6. Take the character of the highest display priority: Passive, then Adaptive, then local enhancement data, then Active, then the Level 1 character. Take the attributes from the Passive object's record when it supplied the character; otherwise from the Adaptive overlay when the cell is in an Adaptive object's range, the later invoked object's where two overlap; otherwise from the underlying record.
  7. Record the cell, update the held mosaic from Level 1 characters only, and record the set-after attributes of the Level 1 byte for the next cell.

The row after a row with spacing double height or double size uses no Level 1 bytes, and only its enhancement events are resolved.

Drawing.

  • The palette mode keeps Level 1 output unchanged at every level, and the resolved mode is chosen at Levels 2.5 and 3.5. The palette mode refuses a grid it cannot draw, and the 4-bit format refuses indices above 15 in the resolved mode.
  • The Level 1 Flash code has the SAA5050's timing, 960 ms on and 320 ms off, in both modes and at every level. The twelve-unit cycle applies only to cells set by the Additional Flash Functions command.
  • Underline is drawn on the last line of the cell, for G0 and G2 characters only, on double height only on the bottom half, enlarged to two lines, and not on empty cells.
  • A transparent colour in row 0, columns 0 to 7, shows the picture, never the row colour. Mix mode makes gaps the picture, so a dot hidden by flash or conceal also shows the picture.
  • Borders are the caller's to fill, in the colour given to it.
  • Still images are drawn in the on phase of every flash.
  • A pixel of a G3 glyph is set when its centre, nudged upwards by an infinitesimal amount, lies inside the shape, which makes mirrors and complements exact. Positions 2/E and 3/E are drawn at half-dots 1 to 2 and 9 to 10, 4/8 to 4/B are straight diagonals, and diagonal strokes are two half-dots wide. The generated glyphs are used at every level.
  • An eight-colour terminal shows each primary when it is above half intensity.

Rests on: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 12.3 Non-spacing attributes and additional characters; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 13 Objects; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 13.2 Display priority order; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 13.8 Default Objects; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 13.9 Object example; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 14 Dynamically Re-definable Characters; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 15 Character sets and their designation; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 10.6.1 Page Format; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 annex A Page addresses; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 annex C Decoding Rules; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause A.4 Addressing order for enhancement triplets; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause A.8 Number of objects per page; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause C.2 Rules for determining priorities of spacing and non-spacing attributes; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table C.1 Display Priorities

7.12 Testing a decoder against the standard

DOC-2121 · reference · informative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-189, REQ-461, REQ-464, REQ-466, REQ-468, REQ-469, REQ-470, REQ-472

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-189 Authoring test pages for Levels 2.5 and 3.5 (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-464 Character mapping against the standard's tables (not implemented on livedevelopment); REQ-468 Level 2.5 and 3.5 enhancement tests (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-470 Object tests (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-472 Bounded work and memory of enhancement processing (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

The standard's own tables and worked examples test a decoder. The engine's tests are built on these, and anyone writing a decoder can use the same list.

  • Hamming 8/4. All 256 byte values against clause 8.2: each of the 16 valid codes decodes to its value, every single-bit error of a valid code is corrected, and every double-bit error is reported as uncorrectable.
  • Characters. The Unicode mapping of every character set and national option against Tables 35 to 48, transcribed once into test data, with every difference explained or fixed.
  • Level 1. Every spacing attribute with its set-at or set-after timing, and every example of Annex G: boxing in G.3.1, separated mosaics in G.3.2 and hold mosaics in G.3.3. Also hold with size changes and with mode changes, double height with its ignored lower row, conceal ended by a colour attribute, New Background after a colour change, and blast-through with national options.
  • Levels 2.5 and 3.5. Every row mode of Table 28 and column mode of Table 29, at both levels, with each class of data value, reserved values included.
  • Worked examples. The three contention examples of Annex C.2, which give red, blue and yellow. The object example of clause 13.9 for each of the Active, Adaptive and Passive types, with the word colours of its notes 1 to 8: its "pink" is CLUT 2 entry 0, red 15, green 0, blue 5, because the standard gives no index. All rows of Table C.1, the precedence lists of Annex A.5, the open area of Figure A.1 and the defaults of Table 30. The layouts of clause 12.5.3 show that side-panel data changes nothing.
  • Objects. Every source and type of invocation and every permitted and forbidden pair of invoker and type; every case in which a pointer, a definition or a link is unusable; each limit of Annex A.8, reached and exceeded; the Magazine Organization Table at both levels, with each Level 3.5 packet missing in turn; default objects with and without local display data; and objects placed past column 39, whose elements are discarded.
  • Bounded work. Worst-case pages: every object type invoked to the limits of Annex A.8, nesting to the greatest depth allowed, objects of 507 triplets and all sixteen X/26 packets. Building the grid stays within a stated bound on operations, uses only the memory the caller gave, and never panics.

Test pages at Levels 2.5 and 3.5 are written by function and not by bit pattern. The engine's builder writes every row and column triplet of Tables 28 and 29 by name; local objects, and POP and GPOP pages with pointer tables computed from their objects; a Magazine Organization Table; and every field of X/28/0 Format 1, X/28/4, M/29/0 and M/29/4; and it writes the result as TTI files and T42 streams. A builder and a decoder written from one reading of the standard share its mistakes, so the builder's output is also checked against the standard's worked examples.

Rests on: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 8.2 Hamming 8/4; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 15.6 Alphanumeric Character Sets; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 15.7 Graphics Character Sets; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 annex G Decoding aspects; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 28 Function of Row Address triplets; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 29 Function of Column Address triplets; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause C.2 Rules for determining priorities of spacing and non-spacing attributes; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 13.9 Object example; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table C.1 Display Priorities; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause A.5 Rules for row and screen colours; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 table 30 Colour Map; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 12.5.3 Data transmission and addressing; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause A.8 Number of objects per page

8 Data carriage and audio

DOC-1785 · section

8.1 What NeoTeletext takes from EN 300 708

DOC-1856 · concept · informative · requirements REQ-40

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-40 Broadcast carriage (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

EN 300 708 defines several ways to send data within teletext. NeoTeletext uses one: the programme-independent audio service on an independent data line, which it declares as a user-defined service and fills with its own records. Sound travels that way, and so does everything else on a live connection that is not a page: the records of the downstream record set.

Part of EN 300 708NeoTeletext
Page Format, clear (clause 4)Such pages are recognised and not decoded.
Page Format, CA (clause 5), IDL Format A (clause 6.5, apart from its check word), Datavideo (clause 6.6), programme-related audio (clause 6.7.2), IDL Format B (clause 6.8), IDL CA (clause 7)Not used.
Programme-independent audio, user-defined (clause 6.7.3)The one carriage of records.

Defined by: EN 300 708 V1.2.1 clause 4 Page Format - Clear; EN 300 708 V1.2.1 clause 6.7 Low bit-rate audio; EN 300 708 V1.2.1 clause 6.7.3 Programme independent audio service; EN 300 708 V1.2.1 table 16 The IDL Data Channels

8.2 Data channel 12

DOC-1857 · reference · normative · requirements REQ-40

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-40 Broadcast carriage (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

Records MUST be carried on independent data lines of data channel 12, designation code 15, which a receiver also sees as packet 4/31. The Service Byte's message bit 4 is set, for a user-defined service, and bits 1 to 3 name one of eight audio services. The Control Byte is Hamming 8/4 coded: bit 1 marks a record's first fragment, bit 2 its last, and bits 3 and 4 are zero, counting from 1 at the least significant bit as EN 300 706 does, so the values are 1 and 2.

Bytes 8 to 45, the 38 audio bytes, hold:

BytesHolds
8Record id, counting new records modulo 256, not repeats
9Fragment index, 0 to 240
10 to 4334 bytes of the record. The last fragment is padded with zeros.
44 to 45A 16-bit check over the 36 bytes before it

The check word divides the data, highest bit of each byte first, by the polynomial of clause 6.5.8.1, from a register of zeros, and sends the remainder high byte first, with no final inversion.

A receiver MUST drop a packet that fails any of this. It MUST reassemble a record from its fragments in any order, and hold a bounded number of records in flight, replacing the oldest incomplete one. An index that disagrees with the first-fragment bit, or at or above 241, is a fault.

The teletext decoder itself ignores packet 31: a port gives packets to this receiver first.

Defined by: EN 300 708 V1.2.1 clause 6.4.1 Designation code; EN 300 708 V1.2.1 clause 6.4.2 Data Channel addressing; EN 300 708 V1.2.1 clause 6.5.8.1 Check word generation; EN 300 708 V1.2.1 clause 6.7.1 Method of coding; EN 300 708 V1.2.1 clause 6.7.3 Programme independent audio service; EN 300 708 V1.2.1 clause 6.7.3.1 Service Byte (SB); EN 300 708 V1.2.1 clause 6.7.3.2 Control Byte (CB); EN 300 708 V1.2.1 clause 6.7.3.3 Audio data; EN 300 708 V1.2.1 table 16 The IDL Data Channels; EN 300 708 V1.2.1 table 21 Service byte for independent audio service

8.3 The record frame

DOC-1858 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-36

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-36 Records (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

Every carriage delivers records, which NeoTeletext defines. A record is a one-byte profile version, 2; a one-byte type; a 16-bit body length; the body; and a CRC-32/ISO-HDLC over the header and body, whose check value for ASCII 123456789 is 0xCBF43926. Integers are big-endian.

An audio record, types 1 to 3, is at most 1,024 bytes with a body of at most 1,016. The frame itself allows a record of 8,192 bytes, 241 fragments at most, for the other records carried with audio. The types of the downstream record set share the frame, and an audio receiver leaves them alone.

TypeRecord
1OBJECT: announce a sound
2DATA: blocks of a sound
3CONTROL: a command
4 to 12The channel's records

A record MUST be refused whole on a wrong length, trailing bytes, an unknown version or type, a failed CRC, or any field outside its range, before anything in it is stored.

8.4 OBJECT, DATA and CONTROL

DOC-1859 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-37, REQ-38, REQ-39

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-38 The DATA record and block geometry (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

OBJECT carries a sound's handle (0 to 255), generation, id, codec, sample-rate code, block size, sample count and block count, then extensions as tag, length and value: a label of at most 64 bytes (tag 1), and a BCP 47 language tag of at most 16 bytes (tag 2). Unknown tags are skipped. The block count MUST equal the sample count divided by the samples of a whole block, rounded up. A sample count of 0 marks a live sound. An object of a reserved codec, or one the build leaves out, is ignored and counted, never a fault.

DATA carries a handle, a generation, the index of its first block, and one or more whole blocks. Blocks of the wrong length, or beyond the sound's end, discard the record.

CONTROL is one command in 14 bytes of body. Flags are 1 progressive, 2 loop and 4 after current, and any other bit refuses the record. The operation is 1 play, 2 stop or 3 set gain. The channel is 0 to 3, the gain 0 to 100 per cent, the fade at most 10,000 milliseconds, and the duck code 0 to 6. The page is a decimal page number 100 to 899 in EN 300 706 form, or 0xFFFF for the whole service.

8.5 Codecs and rates

DOC-1860 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-34, REQ-35, REQ-38, REQ-47, REQ-52, REQ-56, REQ-57

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-38 The DATA record and block geometry (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

Every sound is one channel. A sound's rate MUST be one of the codes of this table, and any other code makes its OBJECT invalid.

Code01234567
Samples a second8,00011,02516,00022,05024,00032,00044,10048,000
CodeCodecA block
0Linear PCM, signed 16-bit little-endianAn even number of bytes, at most 1,000. Only the final block may be shorter.
1IMA ADPCM in WAVE blocks, format tag 0x0011256 or 512 bytes, holding 1 + 2 × (bytes − 4) samples, beginning with its own predictor and step index. Only the final block may be shorter. Decoding follows the IMA's 1992 recommendation, saturates, and refuses a step index above 88.
2Opus, at 48 kHz aloneOne constant-rate packet of 1 to 160 bytes, one length for every block of the sound, holding 960 samples. The final block is whole.
3 to 255ReservedIgnored.

An Opus packet is mono with one 20 millisecond frame, bare or padded in a code 3 packet of one frame. Any other packet is refused before the decoder's state changes. A frame of one byte or none is concealed. Decoding is bit-exact with libopus 1.5.2's fixed-point decoder, with in-band forward error correction not decoded.

An Opus sound's packets are decoded in order by one decoder that follows the sound, unlike PCM and IMA ADPCM blocks. A receiver keeps a bounded number of decoders, and a packet the decoder refuses is concealed and counted.

A receiver is built with the codecs it chooses. The engine's desktop builds decode Opus alone, and its microcontroller builds PCM and IMA ADPCM too. A codec left out is treated as reserved.

Not built. A port cannot yet pass IMA ADPCM blocks undecoded to a sink that decodes them.

8.6 Network and file carriage

DOC-1861 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-41

On a WebSocket, records travel as the packets of data channel 12, whole 42-byte packets in binary messages. A binary message is packets, never a bare record, and one that is not whole packets is refused whole.

A .ttau file is records back to back, with nothing between them. A record whose length breaks the bound ends the file, a truncated final record is discarded, and a framed record that is damaged is refused as on any carriage and the file goes on.

A record never names its service: the host associates each connection or file with one.

8.7 What a receiver does with sound

DOC-1862 · reference · normative · requirements REQ-42, REQ-43, REQ-44, REQ-45, REQ-46, REQ-48, REQ-49, REQ-50, REQ-53, REQ-55

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-42 The audio receiver's object store: sounds kept compressed in a fixed arena, with eviction, generations and conflicts (to be rechecked on livedevelopment); REQ-45 Sounds that belong to a page (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-55 Security (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

Keeping sounds. A receiver keeps sounds compressed in a fixed arena, admitting a sound only if it will fit whole. It evicts the oldest sound no channel has armed or waiting, and reports a resource limit when nothing can go. A repeated OBJECT changes nothing. A newer generation, by RFC 1982 serial arithmetic over 16 bits, replaces the older. An older generation, a different definition of one generation, or a block arriving again with different bytes is a conflict, and a sound in conflict is not played until replaced.

Commands. For each of its four channels a receiver keeps only the sequence number of the last command it accepted, and accepts a command only when its number is newer by RFC 1982 arithmetic over 16 bits. So the latest command wins, a repeat changes nothing, and a stop arriving before the older play it cancels still wins. A change of service clears every channel.

Operations. A play starts its sound on the channel, ramping out what played. With after current it waits for that sound to end, a channel holding one such sound. With loop it repeats until stopped. A stop ramps to silence and drops the waiting sound. Set gain ramps to the new gain. A play whose sound is not ready waits.

Pages. A play naming a page is armed for that page: it starts whenever the page is opened, fades out when it is left, and plays only while the user's page sounds setting is on. A repeat of the record arms nothing new. A play for the whole service plays once, when accepted. Receiving data never starts a sound.

Starting and loss. A play starts once its whole sound has arrived, or with progressive once 40 milliseconds have arrived contiguously from the start. Samples of blocks not yet arrived are silence, and timing is never shifted. A missing block is faded into and out of over 5 milliseconds, its neighbours sent again whole when it arrives, and reported as concealed.

Live sounds. A sound with a sample count of 0 has blocks without end, indexed modulo 216. A receiver holds the newest 32 in a ring, joins at the newest when a play can start, and gives later blocks in index order. A block still missing once three newer ones have arrived is silence, or for Opus is concealed by its decoder. A sink follows the sender's clock, absorbing at least ±1 per cent of error. After two seconds with no block the sound fades out over 100 milliseconds, as clause 6.7.1.1 asks, and the play starts again at the newest block when blocks return.

Levels. Gain is a whole percentage. No level changes faster than over 5 milliseconds. A play's duck code lowers every other channel, while it plays, by Table 19's attenuation applied as a whole percentage:

Duck code0123456
Attenuation, dB04812182430
Per cent1006340251363

A receiver with fewer than four voices plays the lowest-numbered channels, and ignores commands for the rest.

Security. Every audio input is untrusted. A parser MUST bound every length before use. A label is shown only after the cleaning of page text. No sound starts without a command.

Defined by: EN 300 708 V1.2.1 clause 6.7.1.1 Decoder action; EN 300 708 V1.2.1 table 19 Fade byte

8.8 Decided, not built: a receiver's audio sizes

DOC-2125 · reference · informative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-51

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-51 Audio receiver limits: each port sizes and publishes its sound store, objects held and records in flight (not implemented on livedevelopment).

Not built. The engine's receiver takes its sizes from the port that builds it, and defines none of these. A port is to size its store, the objects it holds and the records it has in flight, and publish them. A microcontroller receiver is to hold at least 16 KB of sounds, 8 objects and 2 records in flight, and a desktop receiver at least 4 MB, 256 objects and 8 records. The microcontroller figures are proposals until such a build exists.

9 The channel format

DOC-1786 · section

9.1 A channel's address and boundary

DOC-1863 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-507, REQ-510, REQ-512, REQ-515, REQ-1883

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-507 Channels: a channel is a folder (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-512 Channel addresses (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-515 The channel is the boundary (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-1883 A channel is a folder. (to be rechecked on main).

A channel's address MUST be written in one canonical form. The scheme is http or https. The host is in lower case. The port is written only when it is not the scheme's default. There is no query and no fragment. The folder path begins and ends with /, with at most 8 segments and at most 256 characters in all. Each segment is 1 to 64 characters of letters, digits, -, ., _ and ~, is never made only of dots, and holds no percent-encoding. An address that breaks one of these is refused, naming the rule, and never corrected. The root folder is a channel like any other.

A folder inside a channel's folder is a different channel, and inherits nothing. An address lies inside a channel when it has the channel's origin and its path starts with the folder's. A path holding %2E, %2F or %5C after the folder lies outside it.

The boundary. Each channel has its own cookie store, under the rules of the web. A cookie set by a response inside the folder, or by the push handshake, belongs to the channel. A Path outside the folder is narrowed to it. A response from outside the folder sets no cookie. An address that is a page file in the channel's folder is a page of the channel. Every other address leads to another channel, and is reported to the port. A redirect that leaves the channel is reported and not followed, an upgrade to https on the same host and folder staying inside it, with at most five redirects.

The rules that concern a server as a whole, such as the mark of plain HTTP, no downgrade and the local-network checks, use the origin.

9.2 The files of a channel

DOC-1864 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-497, REQ-508, REQ-1725, REQ-1884, REQ-1887, REQ-1888, REQ-1893, REQ-1904

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-497 Security baseline (to be rechecked on livedevelopment); REQ-508 The files of a channel (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-1725 An ordinary web browser that visits a NeoTeletext resource is answered with HTML, never with raw channel data or an error (to be rechecked on main); REQ-1884 Fixed names, one map. (tested, not recorded as met on main); REQ-1887 Sub-page files. (tested, not recorded as met on main); REQ-1888 The list in parts. (tested, not recorded as met on main); REQ-1904 Bounded readers. (tested, not recorded as met on main).

A channel's files have these fixed names in its folder, and a receiver asks for no others. NNN is a page number as three hexadecimal digits, magazine first, with A to F in capitals, SSSS a sub-code as four such digits, and P a part number in decimal, 0 to 127, with no leading zeros. A page's sub-pages, when they are files of their own, are in a folder named for the page, so that the path of a sub-page's file is its address (page folders and sub-page files).

FileHoldsLargest
channel.ttxThe channel's settings16 KiB
contents.txtIts sections, pages and linked channels64 KiB, 1,000 lines
index.t42Any number of pages as T42, only first sub-pages of a page that has a folder256 KiB
NNN.ttiPage NNN as TTI, with its sub-pages256 KiB
NNN.t42Page NNN as T42, with its sub-pages, or its first when the page has a folder256 KiB
NNN/SSSS.t42One sub-page as T423,108 bytes
NNN/SSSS.ttiOne sub-page as TTI256 KiB
NNN/P.subPart P of a page's list of sub-pages4,119 bytes
pushThe live connection, a WebSocket. One message is at most 64 KiB.Not a file

The sizes are a receiver's defaults, and anything larger is abandoned with a diagnostic. The name channel.ttx is expected to change, and is one named constant in each repository.

What each file may fill. A page's or sub-page's own file fills only pages asked for in this channel, and enhancement pages, whose numbers are not decimal; any other page in it is dropped with a diagnostic. index.t42, a pushed message and a local file fill every page they hold. A part of a page's list fills no page.

For a web browser. neottx html also writes index.html in a channel's folder and NNN/index.html for each page, so that a web visitor finds a page there and not the host's 404. A host serves a file at its folder with no configuration. They are not a channel's files: no reader asks for them, and a channel without them is still a channel. Each NNN/index.html is noindex and holds nothing of the page unless the channel names that page with --public, and all it then shows is readable by anyone.

Not built. More is decided: magazine lists as M.pag, EP1 files as NNN.ep1, and a presentation member in channel.ttx. The structure decided and not built and the items after hand-written and built channels give it. No receiver or server reads or writes any of them, and a member presentation is refused as unknown today.

Rests on: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 9.6.1 Packets X/27/0 to X/27/3 for Editorial Linking

9.3 channel.ttx

DOC-1865 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-491, REQ-509, REQ-513, REQ-1894, REQ-1895

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-509 Aliases and refresh (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-513 `channel.ttx`, a channel's entry file: its settings, as JSON (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-1894 `channel.ttx`. (tested, not recorded as met on main).

channel.ttx holds the channel's settings as JSON, read by a strict, bounded reader of at most 16 KiB. Every member is optional, and an absent one takes its default. A duplicate member refuses the file. An unknown member refuses the file, and the channel does not open.

MemberHoldsDefault
nameThe channel's name for itself, at most 64 bytes of UTF-8. A receiver may show it outside the page, never in row 0.Its host and folder
initial_pageThree decimal digits, the magazine 1 to 8 first"100"
pushAn object, whose presence makes the channel live. Its one member is input, a JSON boolean, by default false: true when the channel takes the viewer's keys. Only this file declares input, and a page cannot. The protocol's version is not here: it is agreed on the connection.Not live
regionThe default character set region, 0 to 15: the group of Table 32 for a page that names none, in the place of the receiver's configured region. A page's X/28/0 and its magazine's M/29/0 stand above it, and in neottx so does the user's --region.The receiver's configured region
servicesAt most 16 linked channels: each a name of up to 32 letters, digits and hyphens, for name:mpp links, a display_name of at most 64 bytes of UTF-8, and either channel, a folder's address in canonical form, or descriptor, a service descriptor's address, never bothNone
aliasesAt most 16 members, each mapping one page number to name:mpp or an address. A typed number with no link on the page follows its alias, and a page's own link wins.None
refreshSeconds, 5 to 86,400: how often the page on screen is asked for again when the channel is not live, conditionallyNever

page_template, push.address, push.method, font and form_origins are refused. A channel's names are fixed, and the WebSocket is the only live connection, always at the folder's push: wss on an https channel and ws on an http one.

A descriptor given by any other address is a service with a page address template, and is accepted only as application/json. This form is what a channel's descriptor link names. The rules of the file's media type are in media types and checks.

9.4 Opening a channel

DOC-1866 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-507, REQ-1898

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-507 Channels: a channel is a folder (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-1898 Opening any address. (tested, not recorded as met on main).

Given a channel's address, a receiver makes these requests in this order, each relative to the folder, and no others:

  1. channel.ttx. With 200 and a valid file, the channel takes its settings. With 404 or 410, it takes the defaults. Any other answer, a server error, a refused media type or an invalid file among them, is a failure, and the channel does not open: the defaults never replace a file that exists but cannot be used.
  2. When the settings have push, the channel is live. The receiver opens the connection and asks for nothing more, and reports the channel as connecting until the initial page arrives, never the page as not found.
  3. Otherwise, index.t42. With 200 and whole T42 packets, every page it holds is stored. With 404 or 410 there is no bundle. Any other failure is reported, and the channel opens without it.
  4. The initial page, from the store or from its own file.

contents.txt is asked for only when the channel's listing is wanted, and never for a live channel, whose contents arrive on its connection.

9.5 Finding a page and a sub-page

DOC-1867 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-492, REQ-493, REQ-498, REQ-508, REQ-1886, REQ-1890

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-492 Fetching pages (to be rechecked on livedevelopment); REQ-508 The files of a channel (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-1886 Finding a page. (tested, not recorded as met on main).

A page is looked for in the store, when it is there and confirmed current; then in NNN.tti; and when that answers 404 or 410, in NNN.t42. Otherwise it is not found. Within a channel, the extension that last answered 200 is tried first. That changes only the number of requests: when the first answers 200, the other is not asked for. A file that answers 200 and fails its check is a failure of that page, and the other extension is not tried.

A sub-page asked for by its sub-code is looked for in the store; then, for a page whose list last answered, in NNN/SSSS.t42 and NNN/SSSS.tti and nowhere else, where 404 or 410 from both means not found; otherwise, for a page with no list or for sub-code 3F7F, in the page's own file. The extension that last answered for a sub-page is tried first, and T42 before any has, so that on a built channel a sub-page is one request. A page's list is asked for only when wanted, a part at a time: part 0 answering 404 or 410 means the page has no folder, and any part answering means it has. A page that has a folder should have no NNN.tti, which would be found first and carry every sub-page whole.

Keeping current. A receiver honours caching headers. Before fetching again it MUST make a conditional request when it holds a validator, and a 304 marks the stored page current. Requests are limited by a configurable rate, and go to the file that answered. A 404 means the page is not in this channel. Other failures are reported without disturbing what is stored. A fetched page remains viewable with the network lost.

9.6 contents.txt

DOC-1868 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-514

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-514 `contents.txt` (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

contents.txt lists a channel's sections, pages and linked channels, in the order shown, and is read by a strict reader. It is at most 64 KiB and 1,000 lines, and a larger file is refused whole. Lines end with LF or CR LF, and fields are separated by one horizontal tab. A byte outside printable ASCII, other than the tab and line ends, makes its line invalid. Empty lines and lines beginning # are comments.

KindFields after itMeaning
stitleA section heading
ppage, title, and optionally a date YYYY-MM-DDA page of this channel, three decimal digits
xtarget, titleAnother channel or a page of one: name:mpp, a folder, or a page file

A title is 1 to 120 characters, and a longer one is cut. A line of an unknown kind is ignored, so later versions may add kinds. A line of a known kind that is malformed, a second p entry for a page, and any p entry past the 800th are ignored and counted. Nothing in the file is checked against the channel's files. It lists pages, never sub-pages.

9.7 Page folders and sub-page files

DOC-2038 · reference · normative · NeoTeletext's own: no standard defines it · requirements REQ-1885, REQ-1887, REQ-1896

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1885 Page folders. (tested, not recorded as met on main); REQ-1887 Sub-page files. (tested, not recorded as met on main); REQ-1896 Derived files. (tested, not recorded as met on main).

Page folders. Inside a channel's folder, a folder named by a page number, three hexadecimal digits with the magazine 1 to 8 first and A to F in capitals, is that page's folder, and belongs to the channel. It is read in either case, so 2fa is a page's folder as 2FA is, because the URL map reads an address's digits in either case and writes them as capitals. A folder named contents or wall cannot be a channel either, and belongs to the channel above it, because a viewer's address <channel>contents must not also be able to name a channel. Any other folder inside a channel's folder is a channel of its own and inherits nothing, and only the end of a path is read, so /100/news/ is a channel. A file's path in a page's folder is the address of what it holds: 602/0101.t42 is what the address 602/0101 names. A folder named as a place of the map holds no other file of a channel, so 602/100.t42 and 602/channel.ttx are no channel's files.

Sub-page files. When page NNN has more than one sub-page, a built channel holds each of them, the first included, as NNN/SSSS.t42, one sub-page's packets and nothing else. NNN.t42 then holds the first sub-page only, so a reader that knows only pages still shows the page. A hand-written sub-page may be NNN/SSSS.tti. SSSS is 0000 to 3F7E: 3F7F names no particular sub-page, so it has no file.

Size. A sub-page's T42 file is at most 74 packets of 42 bytes, 3,108 bytes: the header and rows 1 to 25, 26 packets, and packets X/26, X/27 and X/28 with each of their 16 designation codes, 48 packets. A TTI file is text of no fixed length, and has a page file's largest. A larger file is refused whole.

What a reader does with them. Keying a sub-code asks for NNN/SSSS.t42, then NNN/SSSS.tti, with the extension that last answered first. A 404 or 410 from both means that sub-page does not exist, and the page's own file is not tried, since it holds the first sub-page only. A page's list says whether a page has a folder. A sub-page's file is the truth when it disagrees with its row: one whose validator differs is used, and a listed sub-page whose files answer 404 does not exist.

9.8 NNN/P.sub, a page's list of sub-pages

DOC-1869 · reference · normative · requirements REQ-523, REQ-1888, REQ-1890

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-1888 The list in parts. (tested, not recorded as met on main).

A page whose sub-pages are files of their own has its list of them in its folder, in parts of 64 rows, ROWS_PER_PART. Part P, NNN/P.sub, holds the rows at places 64 × P to 64 × P + 63, counting from 0, and a page of 8,190 sub-pages has parts 0 to 127. A reader finds the part of place k as ⌊k ÷ 64⌋, so it reaches any row with one request and never needs the whole list.

The format is text, read by a strict reader that allocates nothing. Lines end with LF or CR LF, fields are separated by one horizontal tab, and a byte outside printable ASCII other than the tab and the line ends makes its line invalid. There are no comments.

  • Line 1, the header: total, the page's number of sub-pages in decimal with no leading zeros, 1 to 8,190; then version, the page's version. Every part of one version of a page has the same header, so whichever part arrives first sizes a reader's scrollbar.
  • Every further line is one row.
  • Rows are in ascending sub-code order, in a page's file, in its folder's lists and as a reader steps through them, so order is no separate fact. A generator given sub-pages in another order sorts them. A part other than the last holds exactly 64 rows, and the last holds the rest.
Field of a rowHolds
Sub-codeFour capital hexadecimal digits, S4 first, 0000 to 3F7E with no bit outside the thirteen. 3F7F names no particular sub-page, so it has no row.
Validator1 to 16 letters, digits, - and _, which change when the sub-page's file changes
TitleOptional: 1 to 40 characters. A longer one is cut.

Size. The header is at most 4 + 1 + 16 + 2 = 23 bytes, a row at most 4 + 1 + 16 + 1 + 40 + 2 = 64 bytes, and a part at most 23 + 64 × 64 = 4,119 bytes. A larger file is refused whole.

Faults. A header that is missing or invalid makes the part invalid. A row with fewer than two fields or more than three, an invalid sub-code or validator, an empty title field, a sub-code not above the row before it, or a 65th row is ignored and counted. An empty line is a row with too few fields. A part whose version differs from the other parts a reader holds is fetched again, and a reader holding parts of two versions shows neither as current.

What a reader does with it.

  • Stepping to the next or previous sub-page takes the next or previous row of the part it holds, and the part beside it at a part's edge.
  • The list of sub-pages, scrolled, asks for the parts its rows fall in, and the scrollbar is total.
  • A sub-page held whose validator is its row's is current, without asking for its file.
  • When NNN/0.sub answers 404 or 410, the page has no folder, and its sub-pages are those its page file holds.

The engine's writers give each sub-page the first 16 hexadecimal digits, in lower case, of the SHA-256 of its file.

Defined by: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 9.3.1.2 Page sub-code; EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 9.6.1 Packets X/27/0 to X/27/3 for Editorial Linking

9.9 Media types and checks

DOC-1870 · reference · normative · requirements REQ-516, REQ-1903

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-516 Media types and content checks of a channel's files (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-1903 Media types. (tested, not recorded as met on main).

No teletext media type is registered, and hosts send generic ones. A receiver accepts a channel's files with these types, whatever their parameters, for responses with status 200. A 404 or 410 means the file does not exist, whatever its type and body.

FileAccepted media types
NNN.tti, NNN/SSSS.ttitext/x-teletext-tti, text/plain, application/octet-stream
NNN.t42, NNN/SSSS.t42, index.t42application/x-teletext-t42, application/octet-stream, text/plain
NNN/P.sub, contents.txttext/plain, application/octet-stream
channel.ttxAny type but text/html, and none at all. A NeoTeletext server sends application/json.

For every file but channel.ttx, any other type, and no type, is refused with a diagnostic naming the file and the type. Nothing maps the extension .ttx to a type, so a host sends whatever it sends for anything unknown, and the strict reader decides.

Each file MUST then pass the check for the format its name says, and is refused otherwise, never read as another format. A TTI file has at least one PN line. A T42 file is a whole number of 42-byte packets, each with a valid magazine and row address. The others pass their own readers. A refused page file is a failure of that page, not "not found".

Defined by: EN 300 706 V1.2.1 clause 8.2 Hamming 8/4

9.10 TTI page files

DOC-1871 · reference · normative · requirements REQ-177, REQ-178, REQ-182, REQ-183, REQ-187, REQ-188, REQ-1640

Unfinished. The project's records do not show all of this as met, so it may not hold in full: REQ-177 TTI page files (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment); REQ-188 Encoding the packets of Levels 2.5 and 3.5 (tested, not recorded as met on livedevelopment).

A TTI file is text. Lines end with CR LF or LF, and a command is recognised by its first two letters. A PN line starts a new sub-page.

CommandHandling
PN,mppssPage number and sub-page
SC,hhhhSub-code in hexadecimal
PS,hhhhPage status. 0x4000 is C4, 0x0001 C5, 0x0002 C6, 0x0004 C7, 0x0008 C8, 0x0010 C9, 0x0020 C10 and 0x0040 C11. Bits 7, 8 and 9 are C12, C13 and C14.
OL,n,textOutput line for rows 0 to 25
OL,26 to OL,29Enhancement, link and magazine packets. The first character's low four bits are the designation code, and each following three characters carry 18 bits, six a character, least significant first. X/27/0 to X/27/3 carry four-bit values. An OL,29 line on a page numbered mFF is a magazine packet.
FL,Six three-digit hexadecimal links, 0 and 8FF meaning none
LN, LK, LTThe TTI Link Extension. LN takes precedence over FL.
LG,level,textA log line for the channel's author: E, W or I, and 1 to 200 bytes of UTF-8 with no control or bidirectional formatting character
RE,hRegion, which sets the character set group
PF,f,cPage function and page coding
DE,text, DS,textDescription, kept as metadata. DE titles a page.
CTCycle time, ignored
OthersIgnored with a diagnostic

In OL text a control code may be the byte plus 0x80; the escape byte 0x1B followed by the code plus 0x40; or for double height 0x10, read as 0x0D. A writer uses the escape form, so that files are plain text.

Rows of enhancement pages follow their page coding: coding 0 is 40 characters with parity added on encoding; coding 2, for object pages, has a first character whose low four bits give byte 6, then thirteen triplets as for X/26; coding 3, for the MOT, is 40 four-bit values in characters 4/0 to 4/F.

A smallest page file, in which ss is a sub-page number in decimal, SC the full sub-code, and OL,0 starts at column 0, whose first eight characters are not shown:

DE,A page
PN,10000
SC,0000
OL,0,XXXXXXXXA HEADER
OL,1,HELLO
